A new arXiv paper (2609.19524v2) proposes a unified evaluation framework that connects output-level, trajectory-level, and cross-modal assessment across eight trustworthiness dimensions: capability, robustness, safety, fairness, transparency, governance, oversight, and efficiency. It adds a meta-evaluation layer that examines the validity, reliability, and reproducibility of the evaluation itself, and maps results to governance frameworks, international standards, and EU regulatory requirements. Current AI assessment relies heavily on benchmark scores, which give an incomplete picture of trustworthiness, especially for agentic and multimodal systems whose behavior unfolds over time or across modalities. A common framework with uncertainty estimates and traceable evidence could help developers, auditors, and regulators compare systems and verify that safety-critical failures are not hidden by aggregate scores. The framework preserves system-specific metrics while mapping native measurements to common performance bands, and includes safety-critical overrides so aggregate scores cannot mask critical failures. The authors note that empirical validation across real deployment contexts remains an essential next step, and the paper is a preprint without peer review.

## [Paper Argues Multi-Agent Collaboration Only Helps in Sparse-Dependency Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19759) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.19759v2) systematically analyzes when multi-agent collaboration outperforms single-agent systems, concluding that benefits appear mainly in long-horizon tasks with sparse dependencies, while single-agent harnesses remain superior in tightly coupled sequential workflows. The authors propose SAIGE (Semantic-Aware Incremental Graph Evolution), a lightweight mechanism that models collaboration as a dynamically evolving graph with on-demand agent nodes and semantic dependency edges. This work challenges the widespread assumption that adding more agents always improves system intelligence, offering a task-structure-based criterion for deciding when multi-agent designs are worth their growing context overhead. It could directly influence how practitioners architect LLM-based autonomous systems and how agent frameworks like Amazon Bedrock's multi-agent collaboration are deployed. SAIGE encodes semantic dependencies via content-based information retrieval and spawns agents on demand, achieving a favorable trade-off between context efficiency and task performance on long-horizon benchmarks. Notably, the paper reports that scaling the agent pool or deepening recursion levels does not consistently improve outcomes, suggesting multi-agent superiority is bounded by task structure rather than universal.

Graph-based methods model relationships between entities as nodes and edges, and incremental graph evolution updates such structures as new information arrives rather than recomputing from scratch.

Researchers introduced Conservative Discrete Quantile Actor-Critic (CDQAC), an offline reinforcement learning algorithm that learns effective job shop scheduling (JSP) and flexible JSP (FJSP) policies directly from static, suboptimal datasets. Experiments show CDQAC matches or outperforms data-generating heuristics and recent offline/online RL baselines, requiring only 1–5% of the original dataset to learn high-quality policies. This work addresses the high sample-inefficiency of online RL for combinatorial scheduling, showing that offline RL can learn strong policies from suboptimal data. It suggests that state-action coverage matters more than trajectory quality, potentially simplifying data collection for industrial scheduling applications. CDQAC couples a quantile-based critic with delayed policy updates to estimate the return distribution of machine-operation pairs. Notably, datasets generated by a simple random heuristic with broader coverage outperform those from stronger heuristics like Genetic Algorithms, and the source code is publicly available on GitHub.

Online RL methods learn scheduling policies via direct environment interaction but require extensive training, whereas offline RL learns from fixed datasets without further interaction.

## [LLMs Are Shannon Lossy Compressors, Not Solomonoff Induction Estimators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05280) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2601.05280v5) argues that cross-entropy, negative log-likelihood, and other next-token objectives cannot by themselves implement Solomonoff induction, because they optimize fit to a supplied conditional distribution rather than a program-weighted universal mixture. The author contends that adding more compute within a fixed objective improves fit without changing the underlying inductive principle, and that reaching an AI singularity requires symbolic model synthesis via neurosymbolic approaches already being adopted by frontier models such as Fable and Astra. This paper offers conceptual clarity at the intersection of algorithmic information theory and machine learning, challenging the assumption that scaling LLMs alone will lead to AGI or a singularity. It could influence the direction of neurosymbolic research and how the field frames the limits of purely statistical next-token prediction. The paper acknowledges that the data-processing inequality and Levin non-growth remain valid, but argues that for finite learners and finite observers these theoretical boundaries have less relevance, producing divergence rather than violation of information conservation laws. It interprets different resource-bounded estimators as finite tools for mechanism search, and notes that frontier models like Fable and Astra already embrace symbolic computation, so they can no longer be considered purely statistical LLMs.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Solomonoff induction is a mathematical theory of inductive inference that evaluates models by their description length, assigning higher prior probability to shorter algorithms that generate observed data; it formalizes Occam's razor but is incomputable, though approximable with more compute. Algorithmic information theory (AIT) studies the relationship between computation and information, combining Shannon's information theory with Turing's computability theory. Neurosymbolic AI combines neural networks with symbolic reasoning to improve reliability and generalization, and has been called the third wave of AI.

## [HERMES: Risk-Aware Multimodal System for Long-Tail Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00993) ⭐️ 7.0/10

HERMES is a new end-to-end autonomous driving framework that explicitly injects long-tail semantic knowledge into trajectory planning using vision-language models. It builds structured Long-Tail Scene Context and Long-Tail Planning Context via a foundation-model-assisted annotation pipeline, then fuses multi-view images, ego-motion history, and semantic instructions in a Tri-Modal Driving Module for risk-aware trajectory generation. Long-tail, safety-critical scenarios remain the biggest obstacle to deploying autonomous driving at scale, and HERMES shows that combining vision-language semantics with risk-aware planning can consistently outperform recent baselines on a large-scale real-world long-tail benchmark. This could accelerate progress toward safer mixed-traffic autonomous systems and inform future vision-language-action driving research. HERMES uses a foundation-model-assisted annotation pipeline to construct hazard-centric scene information, maneuver intent, and risk-aware planning guidance, and ablation studies confirm the complementary roles of its major components. The work is an arXiv preprint (2602.00993v2) evaluated on a large-scale real-world long-tail driving benchmark, but no community discussion is available yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: End-to-end autonomous driving models map sensor inputs directly to driving actions, and recent work increasingly uses large vision-language models to add semantic understanding. Long-tail scenarios are rare but safety-critical situations, such as unusual road users or near-miss interactions, that standard datasets underrepresent. Risk-aware planning aims to make trajectory generation safer under uncertainty, and HERMES combines these threads into a single holistic framework.

## [MENASpeechBank: A Persona-Conditioned Voice Bank for AudioLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07036) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced MENASpeechBank, a reference speech bank of roughly 18,000 high-quality utterances from 124 speakers across multiple MENA countries, covering English, Modern Standard Arabic, and regional Arabic dialects. Using a controllable pipeline, they generated about 417,000 persona-conditioned multi-turn role-play conversations with speaker-conditioned synthetic user-turn audio, and plan to release the dataset on Hugging Face. AudioLLMs are currently bottlenecked by a lack of diverse, conversational, and instruction-aligned speech-text data, especially for persona-grounded and dialectal interactions. A large, openly released MENA-focused resource could accelerate research on dialect robustness, speaker diversity, and personalized speech assistants that serve underrepresented linguistic communities. The pipeline builds persona profiles enriched with World Values Survey (WVS)-inspired attributes, defines a taxonomy-driven set of about 5,000 conversational scenarios, matches personas to scenarios via semantic similarity, and generates role-play dialogues where the user speaks as the persona and the assistant acts as a helpful agent. Synthetic user-turn audio is produced from reference recordings to preserve speaker diversity, and the paper evaluates both synthetic and human-recorded conversations.

**Background**: AudioLLMs are models that follow natural-language instructions over speech and general audio, analogous to text-based LLMs but with audio understanding and generation. Persona-conditioned dialogue means conversations are shaped by a defined user identity, background, and values, which helps models learn personalized and context-aware responses. The World Values Survey is a long-running global research project measuring people's values and beliefs across nearly 100 countries, and its attributes are used here to make personas culturally grounded. ## [Position Paper Urges Dynamical Systems Perspective for Time Series Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16864) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv position paper (2602.16864v3) argues that the time series modeling field should adopt a dynamical systems (DS) perspective, leveraging DS reconstruction (DSR) methods to infer surrogate models of the underlying systems that generate observed data. The authors contend this approach can yield theoretically optimal forecasts, predict long-term statistics, and reduce computational and memory footprints compared to current time series foundation models. This position paper challenges the current hype-driven trend of time series foundation models by arguing that many advances may be superficial without grounding in dynamical systems theory. If adopted, the DS perspective could fundamentally reshape how forecasting models are designed, evaluated, and deployed, particularly for applications requiring long-term statistical predictions or generalization into unseen regimes such as tipping points. The paper reviews central concepts, methods, measures, and models in DS theory and DSR, and concludes with specific suggestions for translating DSR insights into time series modeling. It emphasizes that DS-based models offer domain-independent theoretical insight into mechanisms underlying time series generation, including upper bounds on performance and potential control strategies.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Time series modeling has evolved from early statistical, mainly linear approaches to modern deep learning and time series foundation models such as Google's TimesFM and MOMENT, which are pretrained on large corpora and can forecast unseen series zero-shot. Dynamical systems reconstruction (DSR) is a class of machine learning methods that aim to infer the governing equations or surrogate models of the underlying dynamical system from observed time series data. While DSR has been explored in fields like reservoir computing and neuroscience, it has not been widely integrated into mainstream time series forecasting pipelines.

The MAMA-MIA Challenge introduces a standardized benchmark for joint breast MRI tumor segmentation and pathologic complete response (pCR) prediction, trained on 1,506 patients from multiple US institutions and evaluated on an external test set of 574 patients from three independent European centers. Twenty-six international teams participated in the final evaluation, which combined predictive performance with subgroup consistency across age, menopausal status, and breast density. This benchmark addresses a critical gap in medical AI: most breast MRI models are developed on heterogeneous datasets with inconsistent protocols, making cross-institutional comparison impossible. By providing standardized datasets, evaluation protocols, and public resources, the challenge enables fair comparison of models and promotes the development of robust, equitable AI systems that generalize across institutions and patient subgroups. The challenge evaluates only pre-treatment imaging for pCR prediction, and results reveal substantial performance variability under a common external evaluation framework, exposing trade-offs between overall accuracy and subgroup fairness. The training cohort comes from US institutions while testing is on European centers, explicitly assessing cross-continental generalization.

## [HEAR Framework Brings Real-Time Sound-Centric Manipulation to Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16086) ⭐️ 7.0/10

An arXiv preprint (2603.16086v2) proposes the Vision-Sound-Language-Action (VSLA) paradigm and the HEAR framework, which integrates streaming audio with vision, language, and proprioception for real-time robotic manipulation. HEAR combines a streaming Historizer, an Envisioner adapted from omni foundation models, an audio world model called Advancer, and a flow-matching Realizer policy, alongside the new OpenX-Sound pretraining dataset and HEAR-Bench benchmark. Existing Vision-Language-Action models treat sound as a static pre-execution prompt or focus only on human speech, leaving robots blind to fleeting environmental acoustics that could verify task state during execution. By formalizing continuous auditory awareness, this work points toward multi-sensory foundation models for embodied agents that can react to dynamic real-world sounds rather than executing open-loop action chunks blindly. The paper identifies the Blind Execution Interval problem, where action chunking with open-loop execution causes acoustic events to be lost between discrete audio observation windows, and argues that robust sound-centric manipulation requires causal persistence and explicit temporal learning. It is an arXiv preprint without peer review, and code and videos are available at https://hear.irmv.top.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take visual and language input and output low-level robot actions, typically built by adding an action decoder to a pretrained vision-language model. Action chunking, popularized by methods such as ACT and Diffusion Policy, predicts and executes sequences of actions in open loop to reduce compounding error, but this creates gaps in perception. Proprioception gives a robot real-time knowledge of its own joint positions and applied forces, and this paper adds streaming audio as a fourth modality alongside vision, language, and proprioception.

A new arXiv paper introduces multilingual story moral generation as a culturally grounded evaluation task, using a dataset of human-written story morals across 14 language-culture pairs. It finds that frontier models like GPT-4o and Gemini produce morals semantically similar to and preferred by humans, yet show markedly less cross-linguistic variation and focus on a narrower set of widely shared values. This matters because it offers a new way to study cultural alignment beyond static benchmarks or knowledge-based tests, showing that high semantic similarity to human answers can mask a loss of cultural diversity. It has implications for AI ethics and for anyone deploying multilingual LLMs in culturally sensitive contexts. The evaluation combines semantic similarity, a human preference survey, and value categorization across 14 language-culture pairs. The authors note that while models approximate central tendencies of human moral interpretation, they struggle to reproduce the diversity that characterizes human narrative understanding.

## [Benchmark Reveals Tokenization Choices Shape Medical Event Model Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16775) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks tokenization strategies for generative medical event models by training 156 models on full hospitalizations using Llama and Qwen architectures. It finds that fusing clinical codes with value deciles into single tokens improves predictive performance across all eight outcome families, with AUROC gains of +0.002 to +0.033 and Spearman correlation gains of +0.025 to +0.114. Tokenization is typically treated as fixed plumbing in medical AI pipelines, yet this study shows it creates an invisible performance ceiling that no amount of additional training can overcome. These findings provide actionable guidance for researchers and practitioners building generative models on electronic health record data, potentially improving downstream clinical prediction tasks. Neither anchoring value bins to reference ranges nor increasing quantization granularity consistently improved performance, and xVal variants underperformed both discrete and soft encodings. Using event-order and admission-relative rotary position embeddings (RoPE) instead of explicit time tokens yielded higher family-mean point estimates across all eight families while reducing input length, and mapping native input to the Common Longitudinal Intensive Care Unit Data Format (CLIF) reduced token count to 28.6% of native sequences while improving performance on six of eight outcome families.

**Background**: Generative medical event models learn by predicting the next token in a patient's timeline, similar to how large language models predict the next word. These models require converting heterogeneous clinical data—diagnosis codes, lab values, vital signs, timestamps—into discrete tokens, but there has been little systematic guidance on how these tokenization decisions affect downstream predictive accuracy. Linear probes, which are simple linear classifiers trained on frozen model representations, are used here to evaluate how informative the learned patient representations are for predicting outcomes in hours 24–48 based on the first 24 hours of hospitalization.

## [REALM Aligns Event Cameras with RGB Foundation Models via LoRA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00271) ⭐️ 7.0/10

REALM introduces a cross-modal framework that projects event camera representations into the pretrained latent space of RGB foundation models using low-rank adaptation (LoRA), as described in arXiv:2605.00271v4. It enables downstream tasks like depth estimation and semantic segmentation by transferring linear heads trained on the RGB teacher, and even allows zero-shot application of frozen image-trained decoders such as MASt3R to raw event data. This work bridges the gap between event cameras and the rich ecosystem of RGB foundation models, potentially unlocking geometric and semantic priors for asynchronous event streams without task-specific training. It could significantly impact event-based vision, multimodal learning, and robotics by enabling generalization across modalities and reducing the need for annotated event datasets. REALM maps events into the ViT-based foundation latent space and demonstrates state-of-the-art performance in wide-baseline feature matching, significantly outperforming specialized architectures. The approach leverages LoRA to bridge the modality gap while keeping the RGB backbone frozen, and code and models are available at https://papers.starslab.ca/realm/.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Event cameras are neuromorphic sensors that asynchronously capture per-pixel brightness changes, offering high temporal resolution, low latency, and robustness to extreme lighting compared to standard frame-based cameras. However, learning-based event processing has been confined to task-specific silos, lacking generalization across modalities. LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that freezes pretrained weights and injects trainable low-rank matrices, enabling adaptation with minimal overhead. RGB foundation models are large pretrained vision models (e.g., ViT-based) that provide strong geometric and semantic priors for image tasks.

## [LLM Agents Suffer 'Constraint Decay' in Backend Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06445) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.06445) from researchers at Eurecom systematically evaluates LLM agents on multi-file backend code generation across 80 greenfield tasks and 20 feature-implementation tasks spanning eight web frameworks. The study identifies a phenomenon called 'constraint decay': as structural requirements accumulate, agent performance drops by an average of 27.28 points in assertion pass rates from baseline to fully specified tasks. This work highlights that jointly satisfying functional and structural requirements remains a key open challenge for coding agents, with direct implications for teams deploying AI-generated backend code in production. The findings suggest that current evaluation benchmarks, which reward functionally correct but structurally arbitrary solutions, may be misleading practitioners about real-world readiness. The study uses a dual evaluation combining end-to-end behavioral tests and static verifiers, and finds that mid-tier models succeed in minimal, explicit frameworks like Flask but perform substantially worse in convention-heavy environments such as FastAPI and Django. Error analysis identifies data-layer defects—including incorrect query composition and ORM runtime violations—as the leading root causes of failures.

Existing benchmarks often overlook these non-functional requirements, creating a gap between benchmark performance and real-world reliability.

## [Diffusion Models Suffer Critical Slowing Down, Paper Shows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12597) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.12597) analytically demonstrates that training a diffusion model's score network on the O(n) model in the Gaussian n→∞ limit exhibits critical slowing down in parameter learning, which also degrades generation quality near criticality. The authors show that a two-layer architecture reduces this slowdown, with training time scaling logarithmically rather than quadratically with system size, and a Fourier implementation achieves this without a large increase in operational complexity. This work offers rare theoretical control over why diffusion models struggle near criticality, a regime that is notoriously hard for both classical and learned samplers. It suggests that architectural depth, not just more compute, can overcome a fundamental sampling bottleneck, with implications for statistical physics simulations and generative modeling more broadly. The analysis is restricted to the analytically tractable Gaussian limit of the O(n) model with n→∞, where the exact score is known and a one-layer network can match it. Under these idealized conditions, critical slowing down appears as quadratic scaling of training time with system size, and the proposed two-layer Fourier architecture reduces this to logarithmic scaling.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models, also called score-based generative models, learn to reverse a noising process by estimating the score (the gradient of the log data density) and then sampling from it. The O(n) model, or n-vector model, is a classic statistical mechanics system of interacting spins used to study phase transitions and critical phenomena. Near a critical point, physical systems exhibit long-range correlations and slow relaxation, a phenomenon known as critical slowing down, which makes conventional sampling algorithms inefficient.

## [ConSPO: A Contrastive Reformulation of GRPO for RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12969) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2605.12969v5) shows that GRPO admits an equivalent discriminative reformulation, exposing two objective-level limitations: likelihood-misaligned surrogate scores and score-insensitive credit assignment. To fix these, the authors propose ConSPO, a Contrastive Sequence-level Policy Optimization method that uses length-normalized sequence log-probabilities as rollout scores and contrasts verified positive rollouts against negative distractors within the same group. GRPO is one of the most widely adopted RLVR algorithms for post-training LLMs on reasoning tasks, so a principled reformulation and improvement could directly affect how models like DeepSeek-R1-style reasoners are trained. ConSPO's reported gains on challenging reasoning benchmarks suggest practical value for researchers working on RL and LLM alignment. ConSPO optimizes a group-wise InfoNCE-style objective that adaptively strengthens updates for poorly separated positives and high-scoring negatives, and adds a curriculum-scheduled margin to preserve separation pressure as training progresses. The paper is a preprint with no community discussion yet, so the results have not been independently verified.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) trains LLMs using automatically checkable reward signals, such as whether a math answer is correct, rather than learned reward models. GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization), popularized by DeepSeek-R1, generates multiple completions per prompt, forms a group, and computes advantages relative to other rewards in that group instead of using a separate value network. Contrastive learning, as used in InfoNCE, trains a model by pulling positive pairs together and pushing negative pairs apart, which ConSPO adapts to the sequence level for policy optimization.

## [PaCo-VLA Adds Passivity Shield to Vision-Language-Action Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00515) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PaCo-VLA introduces a passivity-shielded compliance prior that treats Vision-Language-Action (VLA) model outputs as task-level compliance proposals—semantic bindings, task stages, and admittance schedules—rather than direct motor commands. A high-frequency, proposal-independent passivity shield governs these proposals through energy-tank accounting and boundary checks, and experiments on connector insertion show superior precision over unshielded VLA baselines with zero passivity violations even under adversarial compliance shifts. This work addresses a real reliability gap in force-sensitive tasks by decoupling high-level VLA semantic reasoning from low-level passivity-based control, potentially influencing how future VLA systems are designed for contact-rich domains. It offers a provably sampled-passive runtime contract at the admittance port, which could make foundation models safer to deploy in physical manipulation. The shield is proposal-independent and uses energy-tank accounting to prevent invalid, stale, or unverified model predictions from bypassing low-level contact physics, while also enabling causal evaluation that isolates semantic contributions from geometric shortcuts. The approach is validated in both simulated and real-world connector-insertion experiments, though it remains an arXiv preprint without community discussion or engagement signals.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take an image and a text instruction and directly output low-level robot actions, pioneered by Google DeepMind's RT-2 in 2023. Passivity-based control is a classical robotics approach that relates to energy dissipation, providing natural guarantees of stability and tracking for mechanical systems. Admittance control is a force-control framework that makes a robot behave like a mass-spring-damper system, allowing it to comply with external forces during contact-rich tasks such as insertion.

## [AgenticRL: Self-Refining Multi-Agent Framework Automates UAV Navigation Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03963) ⭐️ 7.0/10

AgenticRL introduces a multimodal closed-loop framework in which role-specialized agents generate executable reward code, diagnose policy failures, and issue targeted refinement instructions to regenerate improved rewards for complex UAV navigation tasks. Evaluated across eight UAV tasks, it achieves 100% success in racing and 88% in cluttered navigation, boosting mean simulation success from 37.2% to 96.4% and reaching a 90.0% real-world success rate. Manual reward design remains one of the most time-consuming bottlenecks in applying deep reinforcement learning to robotics, and AgenticRL's automated diagnosis-driven refinement could substantially reduce that human effort. Its strong sim-to-real accuracy of 93.4% suggests the approach may generalize beyond UAVs to other autonomous systems where reward engineering is a barrier. Before training, a task grounding stage automatically selects a compatible action profile along with its observation and reward interfaces, and each generated reward trains a policy via Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) that is then evaluated under randomized conditions. Task-level behavioral, geometric, and safety measurements are organized into a structured diagnosis packet and jointly analyzed with the reward code, task specification, behavioral summary, and visual scene context.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Deep reinforcement learning lets robots learn complex navigation by trial and error, but it depends heavily on hand-crafted reward functions that are slow to design and tune. Automated reward generation methods like Eureka and Text2Reward reduce this effort, yet they often lack task-level behavioral diagnosis to guide subsequent reward revisions. AgenticRL addresses this gap by using role-specialized agents in a closed loop that diagnoses the behavior induced by a reward and directs its next revision, rather than relying on one-shot generation, human guidance, or broad candidate search.

## [WorldRoamBench: A New Benchmark for Long-Horizon Stability of Interactive World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.31672) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduced WorldRoamBench, an open-world benchmark that evaluates the long-horizon stability of interactive world models (IWMs) across four dimensions: action, vision, physics, and memory. The benchmark includes over 1,000 test cases spanning Nature, Urban, and Indoor scenes in first- and third-person views with 10–60 seconds of continuous WASD interaction, and testing 10+ open- and closed-source models showed that none reliably satisfies all four dimensions. Short-horizon demos often hide failures that only appear during extended interaction, so a standardized long-horizon benchmark gives the field a shared way to measure whether IWMs remain stable, physically plausible, and memory-faithful over time. This could shape how future world models are trained and compared, especially as interest grows in deploying them for real-time interactive applications. The benchmark introduces a per-frame action metric that bypasses cross-model semantic scale disparities, a sliding-window drift metric that captures non-monotonic mid-sequence collapse missed by start-vs-end comparisons, physics evaluation across mechanics, optics, and 3D consistency gated by camera-motion and subject-tracking checks, and a trajectory-aware memory protocol using transition-localized 3D point-cloud reconstruction and tracking-plus-VLM reasoning. Even the best-performing model achieves only moderate scores, indicating substantial room for improvement.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Interactive world models are AI systems that generate explorable environments in response to user actions, and they are central to applications like game generation and embodied simulation. Long-horizon stability refers to whether such a model can maintain consistent behavior, visuals, physics, and memory over extended interaction rather than just a few frames. Vision-language models (VLMs) are multimodal models that reason over both images and text, and here they are used to help judge whether a generated world remembers its subjects correctly.

## [STL-Based Reward Shaping Enables Gait-Aware Quadruped Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.00442) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.00442v2) introduces a framework that specifies distinct quadruped gaits—walking-trot, trot, and bound—using parameterized Signal Temporal Logic (STL) constraints, then converts these specifications into dense reward signals via smooth approximations of STL robustness. The approach, instantiated on Google's Barkour robot in MuJoCo XLA (MJX) and trained with PPO, outperforms hand-crafted rewards, an expert-switching oracle, and Text2Reward in command-tracking success and gait consistency. This work addresses a key limitation of reinforcement learning for legged robots: hand-crafted, Markovian reward functions are hard to interpret and give no explicit control over gait patterns. By using formal STL specifications, the framework offers a more interpretable and controllable way to shape locomotion behaviors, which could improve safety, efficiency, and adaptability in real-world robotic deployments. The framework defines parametric STL templates for three speed regimes, calibrates their parameters from reference rollouts, and computes rewards using smooth approximations of STL robustness that are compatible with Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Training uses parallelization in MJX for speed and domain randomization to robustify policies, though the approach is currently validated only in simulation on the Barkour robot.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Signal Temporal Logic (STL) is a formal specification language for reasoning about temporal properties of real-valued signals in cyber-physical systems, allowing constraints like safety bounds and synchronization to be expressed precisely. STL robustness measures how well a signal satisfies a specification, but exact computation is often non-differentiable; smooth approximations enable gradient-based optimization. Quadruped gaits such as walking-trot, trot, and bound are distinct patterns of leg coordination that animals and robots use at different speeds, and reinforcement learning is a common method for training locomotion controllers.

## [GeoSelect: Training-Free Spatial-Program Execution for Remote Sensing Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03869) ⭐️ 7.0/10

GeoSelect introduces a training-free pipeline that reframes referring remote sensing image segmentation as the execution of a typed spatial program synthesized by a frozen, text-only language model. It achieves 58.86 mIoU on the RRSIS-D test set and 55.27 mIoU on RISBench, more than doubling the best prior training-free method on RRSIS-D without any referring supervision and using only a single GPU. This work shows that explicit, inspectable spatial program execution can outperform implicit vision-language similarity for aerial referring tasks, offering better control over spatial, superlative, and ordinal relations that dominate remote sensing expressions. It could influence how training-free segmentation systems are designed for domains where labeled referring data is scarce. The central abstraction is a single scored candidate set type under which all operators compose: continuous geometric fields handle position and proximity, while discrete set and order operators add extremum, ordinal, top-k, and relational constructions. Execution is explicit so every intermediate is inspectable, and a reliability ladder degrades any failing program to the field-only special case; code and configurations are available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Referring remote sensing image segmentation is the task of segmenting the object named by a natural-language expression in an aerial image, a problem introduced and formalized by the RRSIS line of work. Existing training-free methods typically resolve expressions through implicit vision-language activations or region-text similarity, which gives weak control over spatial relations like 'the rightmost ship' or 'the second court from the left'. GeoSelect instead uses a frozen language model to synthesize a small domain-specific language program, which a well-formedness checker validates before a deterministic executor runs it.

## [Introspection Threshold Proposed for LLM Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04277) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2607.04277v2) argues that sustainable recursive self-improvement in Large Language Models requires a functional analogue to von Neumann's complexity threshold: true introspection, the capacity to simulate one's own operations and target modifications. Grounded in Kleene's Second Recursion Theorem, the authors show such introspective programs theoretically exist, but an empirical review finds current LLMs only exhibit quasi-introspection and fall short due to structural bottlenecks. This work reframes the debate over self-evolving AI by giving a rigorous theoretical criterion for when autonomous self-improvement is sustainable rather than degenerative, which is directly relevant to AI safety and capability forecasting. It suggests that current Transformer-based LLMs cannot bootstrap themselves without architectural changes, affecting how researchers and policymakers think about recursive self-improvement timelines and risks. The paper identifies three structural bottlenecks: a lack of complete self-access, the feedforward nature of the Transformer, and computational class constraints that prevent fixed-point iteration. It concludes by outlining architectural paths to cross this complexity threshold and discusses associated safety implications, though it remains a preprint without extensive community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Von Neumann's universal constructor showed that a self-replicating machine needs a certain minimum complexity to reproduce itself and its description, a result often called the complexity threshold for self-reproducing automata. Kleene's Second Recursion Theorem is a foundational result in computability theory showing that computable functions can be applied to their own descriptions, guaranteeing the existence of self-referential programs such as quines. The paper combines these ideas to ask whether LLMs can genuinely introspect—simulate their own computations—which is necessary for stable recursive self-improvement rather than degeneration.

## [Prompt-Driven Exploration Uses Language as Global Exploration Space for VLA RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08837) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2607.08837v4) proposes Prompt-Driven Exploration (PDE), a method that treats natural language prompts as a global exploration space for reinforcement learning with vision-language-action (VLA) models. In PDE, a vision-language model (VLM) watches rollout videos, diagnoses how the policy responded, and rewrites the prompt to elicit better behavior, enabling RL to learn successful policies even from zero-reward starts. Standard RL exploration injects noise into the action space, which only produces rollouts close to the original policy and cannot escape weak policies in sparse-reward settings. By using language as a global perturbation space, PDE offers a creative solution to a fundamental challenge in RL and could influence future VLA and robotics research. The procedure resembles posterior sampling, a classical RL exploration framework, but operates at the level of prompts: the VLM maintains an implicit distribution over useful prompts and updates it from observed rollouts. Across manipulation and reasoning tasks, PDE improves sample efficiency and enables learning from zero-reward starts; the work is a preprint without peer review yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take visual observations and natural language instructions and directly output low-level robot actions. Reinforcement learning typically explores by adding noise to actions, but such jitter only yields rollouts close to the original policy, so escaping a weak policy often requires global perturbations that action noise cannot produce. Because VLA policies are conditioned on a natural language prompt, modifying that prompt induces global changes in behavior, making language a promising exploration space.

A new arXiv paper (2607.09224v3) introduces Git-Assistant, an AI assistant that combines large language models with automated planning to translate natural language requests into correct and safe Git command sequences. The authors present a systematic evaluation methodology using synthetic and randomized Git environments, comparing LLM-only variants against planning-augmented variants across multiple metrics, and find that adding formal reasoning improves reliability and reduces errors. Git remains notoriously difficult for many developers, especially when performing non-trivial operations like rebasing, cherry-picking, or resolving conflicts, and mistakes can corrupt repository history. This work shows that hybrid AI approaches pairing LLMs with formal planning can make AI developer assistants more trustworthy, which matters for tooling vendors and teams adopting AI-assisted software engineering workflows. The evaluation relies on synthetic and randomized Git environments rather than real-world repositories, so results may not fully capture the messiness of production codebases. Researchers present MetaEvolve, a framework that cultivates self-evolution meta-skills such as self-reflection with environment feedback in LLMs through a data synthesis pipeline, evolution-aware reinforcement learning, and inference-time evolutionary search, grounded in coding tasks. Across seven coding benchmarks, MetaEvolve outperforms the strongest baseline by 10.01% absolute on in-distribution tasks, 24.12% on out-of-distribution tasks, and achieves a 46.9% relative improvement on open-ended algorithm optimization problems outside the training domain. This work suggests that the gains of test-time self-evolution frameworks like AlphaEvolve stem from meta-skills that traditional post-training neglects, offering a principled way to build more autonomous, self-improving AI. If these domain-agnostic meta-skills transfer broadly, they could improve LLM performance on open-ended problems where rich reward signals are scarce. MetaEvolve grounds training in coding, where program execution provides continuous reward signals beyond binary correctness, and synthesizes evolution trajectories containing a current program, a fitness score combining correctness and efficiency, and a history of prior attempts. The model is trained via RL with verifiable rewards derived from test case execution, though the paper is an arXiv preprint and the transfer to non-coding domains remains to be fully validated.

## [Instruction Tuning Boosts Model Confidence but Reduces Rationale Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13430) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2608.13430v2) evaluates three matched base and instruction-tuned models across question-answering benchmarks, finding that instruction tuning consistently raises answer confidence despite limited accuracy gains and degrades likelihood-based calibration. It also reports that cross-rationale diversity consistently decreases, while surface-level lexical diversity varies in both direction and magnitude across models and benchmarks. The findings suggest that instruction tuning, a standard step in building modern assistants, may make models sound more certain without making them more correct, which could mislead users who rely on expressed confidence. It also shows that confidence and rationale diversity capture distinct effects, meaning evaluation and safety work should measure them separately rather than treating them as one phenomenon. The authors control for answer selection and rationale length, confirming that the observed confidence and diversity shifts are not artifacts of which answers were chosen or how long the rationales were. The effect on lexical diversity is non-uniform, so it cannot be summarized as a simple increase or decrease across all settings.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Instruction tuning is a fine-tuning stage in which a language model is trained on labeled instruction-response pairs so it follows user requests more reliably. Confidence calibration refers to how well a model's stated confidence matches its actual probability of being correct, while lexical diversity measures how varied the wording of generated text is. This paper connects these two areas by asking whether instruction tuning changes both at once.

A new arXiv survey (2608.17605v2) reviews multi-turn conversational AI across four system families: text-only dialogue, AudioLLMs and speech-native systems, multimodal/omni-modal systems, and tool-augmented agents. It organizes the literature around datasets and benchmarks, modeling paradigms, training strategies, evaluation setups, and cross-cutting challenges, with an accompanying GitHub repository. The review covers text-only dialogue, AudioLLMs and speech-native systems, multimodal and omni-modal systems, and tool-augmented agents, and concludes with a research agenda for systems that can remember, revise, ground, speak, listen, act, and adapt across turns, modalities, and cultures. It is a literature review rather than a novel technical breakthrough, and no community discussion of the paper is available.

A new arXiv paper (2608.18265v4) introduces a general AI-based modeling technique that assigns a large language model a "type vector" of trait intensities—such as Altruism: 2 out of 5 and Risk Aversion: 4 out of 5—and then asks it to make choices in settings where human decisions are observed. The authors validated the method on 119,147 decisions from 78,657 subjects across more than 35 countries and 10 classic economic game roles, finding that human behavior can be closely matched using just three dimensions: Risk Aversion, Strategic Sophistication, and Trust. This work bridges the flexibility and interpretability of natural language with a mathematical structure that can be fitted to data, offering behavioral economists and AI alignment researchers a practical tool for modeling decision-making across diverse settings. Because individual type vectors can predict behavior in held-out games with different rules and available actions, the approach could generalize far beyond the specific economic games studied. The type vectors needed to fit individuals across games cluster into fewer than a dozen groups, though there is substantial variation in fit quality across subjects. The method is highly generalizable: any vector of traits can be input to model behavior in any setting, and the paper reports that the approach is easy to implement.

## [ESSE: LLM-Powered Self-Explanation Tutor for CS1 Worked Examples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25180) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers built ESSE, an LLM-powered self-explanation tutor for introductory programming (CS1) worked examples, in which students explain individual lines of code and receive immediate LLM feedback on correctness and completeness. The study evaluated the LLM's judgments against two human reference standards—a single domain expert and a crowd of non-expert raters—and deployed the tutor in an introductory Java course, finding that students persisted and revised rather than abandoned lines, produced more complete and conceptually richer explanations across attempts, and showed evidence of learning. Self-explanation is a well-established active-learning technique, but assessing free-text explanations at scale has long been impractical, so this work shows LLM-based assessment may be reliable enough to power automated tutoring. If validated, such tutors could make active study of worked examples feasible in large CS1 courses where instructor feedback cannot scale, potentially improving novice programming outcomes. The evaluation is notable for using two independent human reference standards of different kinds—a single domain expert and a crowd of non-expert raters—each with its own strengths and weaknesses, and the paper characterizes both where the LLM is reliable and the systematic tendencies in how it diverges from human judgments. The deployment evidence comes from an introductory Java course, with outcomes measured as persistence, revision, explanation completeness and conceptual richness across attempts, and learning gains.

**Background**: Worked examples are complete solutions whose lines are annotated with expert explanations, and they help novices in introductory programming (CS1) courses acquire problem-solving schemas before writing code themselves. However, reading expert explanations is passive; self-explanation—where students explain the problem and its solution to themselves with subgoal-level analysis—turns that reading into active study, but it has been hard to scale because assessing free-text explanations and returning timely feedback had no easy automated solution. Large language models (LLMs) are now being explored as a way to fill that gap by judging and responding to student text automatically.

<a id="item-158"></a>
## [Training-Free Self-Embedding Steganography Defends Against Partial Speech Deepfakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25285) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.25285v2) proposes a training-free proactive defense against partial deepfake speech, in which a clean speech signal embeds a compressed representation of itself via audio steganography so that reference content can be extracted after the fact. The authors show that existing audio steganography methods can be repurposed for codec-based restoration, enabling detection and restoration of manipulated segments without any model training. Partial deepfake speech, where only short segments of an utterance are synthesized, is increasingly hard for passive detectors to catch as the spoofed proportion shrinks, so this work offers a complementary, data-efficient defense that could be layered on top of existing detection pipelines. It matters for voice authentication, forensic analysis, and media verification systems that must localize and undo small manipulations rather than just flag an entire utterance. The approach is self-embedding: the clean signal carries a compressed copy of itself, and post-hoc extraction supplies reference content for codec-based restoration of manipulated regions. Experiments on a benchmark dataset indicate the method complements passive defenses, and notably it requires no training, making it robust and data-efficient; however, the abstract is truncated, so full quantitative results and capacity/quality trade-offs are not yet visible.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Audio steganography hides information inside sound by manipulating sample data or frequency content, and self-embedding specifically means embedding a compressed copy of the media into itself, a technique long used for image and video tamper detection. Partial deepfake speech detection is a harder variant of deepfake detection because only short temporal portions of an otherwise genuine utterance are manipulated, which defeats utterance-level classifiers. This paper connects the two ideas, using steganographic self-embedding as a proactive defense that enables post-hoc localization and restoration instead of relying solely on passive spoof classifiers.

<a id="item-159"></a>
## [SARA Attack Defeats Token Shuffling Privacy in ViT Split Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01232) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers introduce the Spatially Aligned Reconstruction Attack (SARA), a unified pipeline that predicts token positions, restores spatial layout, reconstructs missing embeddings with a feature-space masked autoencoder, and recovers the input image from transmitted ViT token embeddings. Experiments on ImageNet-1K with ViT-B/16 and MAE-B/16 show that token shuffling provides only apparent privacy, while token reduction offers stronger but still incomplete protection. This work undermines a common assumption that token shuffling can protect privacy in split inference, a setting increasingly used for edge computing where intermediate representations are sent to remote clouds. It affects researchers and practitioners in privacy-preserving machine learning, edge AI, and cloud inference who rely on such obfuscation techniques. The attack exploits positional information retained in token embeddings, and the paper also proposes a lightweight edge-side defense that removes positional embeddings and progressively adapts edge-side transformer blocks via knowledge distillation, reducing SARA's performance while preserving downstream accuracy without changing the cloud-side model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision Transformers (ViTs) split an image into fixed-size patches treated as a sequence of tokens, similar to words in NLP. In split inference, an edge device runs the first part of the model and sends intermediate token representations to a remote cloud for the rest of the computation, which saves local compute but risks leaking private input data through feature inversion attacks. Token reduction lowers computation and communication by dropping or merging tokens, while token shuffling disrupts their spatial order to limit information leakage.

<a id="item-160"></a>
## [VLA-Precision: Asymmetric Co-Bootstrapping for Real-World Online RL of VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04355) ⭐️ 7.0/10

VLA-Precision introduces the Asymmetric Co-Bootstrapping (ACoB) algorithm and the ACoB-Stream architecture to make real-world online reinforcement learning of vision-language-action (VLA) models more stable and efficient. Evaluated on nine high-precision chemistry tasks across four robot embodiments, it achieves a 98.3% mean success rate at 45.8 minutes per task, with 27.6-second episodes running at 1.2x and 1.8x the speeds of VLA and RL baselines, and up to 10.9x improvements in throughput and computational efficiency. Real-world online RL lets VLA models improve beyond imitation demonstrations, but unreliable value signals and the computational overhead of large VLAs have limited practical deployment. By addressing both bottlenecks, this work could make autonomous trial-and-error learning feasible for precision-critical robotic manipulation in domains such as chemistry and laboratory automation. ACoB couples rapid intervention-guided behavioral learning with progressive value calibration across timescales, using global return propagation and local preference ranking to produce relative action advantages for reference-regularized policy improvement while suppressing drift. ACoB-Stream enables this on large VLAs through invariant-state decoupling and on-demand streaming, though the results come from an arXiv preprint that has not yet undergone peer review.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that combine visual perception, language understanding, and low-level robot action generation, enabling broad manipulation capabilities after pretraining. However, they often remain unreliable on tasks requiring precision and repeatability, and post-training them with real-world online reinforcement learning is difficult because value estimates can be noisy and large models are slow to train. VLA-Precision targets these two issues with a new RL algorithm and a training architecture designed for large models.

## [Intentest Uses Fact-Intent DAG to Boost LLM Penetration Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07344) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2609.07344v2) introduces Intentest, an intent-graph-guided automated penetration testing agent that externalizes long-horizon state from the LLM's context window onto a persistent fact-intent directed acyclic graph (DAG). On a benchmark of real CTF challenges covering more than ten vulnerability types across three difficulty levels, Intentest achieves an overall success rate of 88.2% and 75.0% on hard tasks, improving over the baseline by roughly 44 and 50 percentage points. Context forgetting and intent drift are core bottlenecks for LLM agents on long-horizon security tasks, so externalizing state into a structured graph could make automated penetration testing more reliable and less repetitive. If the approach generalizes, it may influence how LLM-based cybersecurity agents are architected beyond web application testing. In the DAG, verified network states are stored as immutable fact nodes and exploration directions are constrained as intent edges bounded by predecessor facts, while a three-layer architecture handles fact-intent mapping, task scheduling with two-phase degradation recovery and adaptive load balancing, and intent retrieval via a top-down five-stage filtering algorithm. Ablation experiments show the intent retrieval and prediction layer cuts average rounds on successful medium and hard tasks by about 33% and 48% respectively, without changing the set of solvable tasks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: LLM-based agents are increasingly applied to cybersecurity tasks such as vulnerability discovery and automated penetration testing, but on long-horizon tasks they tend to lose early critical facts and causal reasoning chains, falling into aimless repetitive exploration. A directed acyclic graph (DAG) is a graph with directed edges and no cycles, often used to represent dependencies or causal structure; here it serves as an external, persistent memory of verified facts and pending exploration intents. Automated penetration testing of web applications is a representative long-tail security task, and CTF (Capture The Flag) challenges are commonly used as realistic benchmarks for it.

## [Fine PT-PT Web: 41B-Token European Portuguese Corpus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.07699) ⭐️ 7.0/10

The paper introduces an efficient pipeline that curates a production-ready 41-billion-token European Portuguese (PT-PT) corpus from 411 TB of raw data sourced from Arquivo.pt. Its novel post-scraping block removes boilerplate and line duplicates before filtering, increasing final document yield by 19.04% by rescuing valid text that standard heuristic filters would otherwise discard. European Portuguese is a low-resource language variant whose web data is heavily confounded by Brazilian Portuguese (PT-BR), so a clean, large-scale PT-PT corpus directly addresses a genuine data scarcity problem for LLM pre-training. The scalable, well-documented pipeline offers a reusable framework for curating corpora for other regional language variants facing similar dialectal overlap. The pipeline integrates rigorous language identification, weighted fuzzy deduplication, and neural quality classification to produce a clean, representative corpus optimized for LLM pre-training. The 19.04% yield gain comes specifically from the early-stage post-scraping intervention, which operates before the standard heuristic filtering steps.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Arquivo.pt, formerly the Portuguese Web Archive, is a non-profit service that has preserved Portuguese web content dating back to 1996 and, as of March 2025, stores over 21 billion webpages totaling 1.4 petabytes. Curating web corpora for regional language variants like European Portuguese is difficult because of dialectal overlap with Brazilian Portuguese and the sheer scale of processing raw web data. LLM pre-training typically requires massive, high-quality text datasets, and standard heuristic filters often discard valid documents along with boilerplate and duplicates.

## [SinkProbe Tests Whether New Attention Fixes Sinks at 1M Tokens](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08574) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.08574v2) introduces SinkProbe, a diagnostic suite that measures sink mass, massive activation, position-resolved recall, and the recency gap, and applies it to four small models that differ only in how they mix tokens and depth. The study finds that the training objective, not the architecture, produces the attention sink, and that gated attention did not reproduce its published effect (cutting first-token attention from 46.7% to 4.8%) at the tested scale. The result challenges the assumption that architectural innovations like gated attention are sufficient to fix attention sinks, suggesting that long-context models advertising million-token windows may still waste budget on the first token because of their training objective. This has direct implications for how long-context LLMs such as Kimi K3 are designed and evaluated. SinkProbe measures sink mass, massive activation, position-resolved recall, and the recency gap, and the paper reports that these three quantities moved independently across the four models. The code, data, and measurement protocol are released at a public GitHub repository, and the study is a preprint without peer review or community discussion yet.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Attention sinks are a known phenomenon in autoregressive language models where attention heads with nothing useful to read still spend their budget on the first token, which can hurt long-context performance. Gated attention is an architecture that uses trainable, context-conditioned multiplicative gates to modulate attention distributions, and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) is a linear attention mechanism that refines Gated DeltaNet with fine-grained diagonal gating. Kimi K3 reportedly pairs gated attention with KDA and Attention Residuals behind a one-million-token window, eight times past the range where these diagnostics have been reported.

## [New Bounds Show Linear Accessibility in Feature Superposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.09556) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.09556v2) derives high-probability bounds for linear accessibility in feature superposition by framing it as a compressed sensing problem, proving the sufficient dimension scales linearly as d = O_ε(k log m) rather than the prior worst-case quadratic limit. It also introduces IHT-SAE, a learned iterative refinement method that improves feature recovery beyond the limits of linear availability. This result quantifies the geometric constraints of the linear representation hypothesis, offering a theoretical framework for evaluating sparse autoencoders, compositional generalization, and neural interpretability. It matters for mechanistic interpretability researchers who need principled guarantees about when features encoded in superposition can be reliably recovered. The analysis characterizes the asymmetry between active and inactive feature interference and the trade-off between interference and observation-noise budgets under subgaussian noise, validating the bounds through Gaussian-tail approximations. IHT-SAE uses learned iterative refinement to push recovery beyond what linear accessibility alone permits.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Feature superposition is the phenomenon where neural networks encode more concepts than they have dimensions, which helps explain polysemantic neurons but introduces cross-feature interference. Compressed sensing is a signal processing technique that recovers sparse signals from far fewer samples than the Nyquist–Shannon theorem requires, relying on sparsity and incoherence. Sparse autoencoders are autoencoder variants whose codes are mostly zero, widely used to extract interpretable features from neural activations.

<a id="item-165"></a>
## [On-Policy Distillation Found Nearly Data-Agnostic, Eight Prompts Match 17k Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14193) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.14193v2) reports that on-policy distillation (OPD) is almost indifferent to its training data: just eight prompts match the performance of a 17k-problem dataset, and three independently built datasets with several-fold differences in difficulty and teacher-student KL produce nearly identical training curves. The authors push this to the limit with Data-free On-policy Distillation (DF-OPD), where the teacher generates its own training questions under a simple prompt with no external data or quality filtering, matching or surpassing real data; in multi-teacher distillation, 1k self-generated questions close 98.5% of the available headroom, beating the 96.6% achieved with 7k real examples. This finding challenges the common assumption that OPD requires large, carefully curated datasets, and could substantially reduce the data engineering cost of frontier post-training pipelines. It also suggests OPD transfers the teacher's mode of reasoning rather than domain-specific knowledge, which has practical implications for how practitioners design distillation data and multi-teacher setups. The paper attributes the data insensitivity to two causes: the unit of data in OPD is the state a prompt leads to rather than the prompt itself, so a single prompt keeps exposing new teacher corrections as sampling continues while the marginal value of additional prompts collapses after eight; and replacing mathematics with competitive programming still recovers over 90% of the in-domain gain. DF-OPD's self-generated questions track the teacher's own post-training data on three key diagnostics of training dynamics, which other real datasets do not.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: On-policy distillation (OPD) is a knowledge distillation technique for large language models in which the student model generates its own trajectories and a teacher model provides dense token-level supervision at the student-visited states, reducing the train-inference distribution gap of off-policy distillation. It has become a standard component of frontier post-training pipelines for transferring capabilities from large teachers to smaller, deployable students. Teacher-student KL divergence is commonly used as the distillation loss that measures how closely the student matches the teacher's output distribution.

## [Review Paper Outlines Evaluation Methods for Healthcare LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14819) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv review paper (2609.14819v2) provides a structured framework for evaluating large language models in healthcare, organized around four areas: study design principles, statistical methods, capability evaluation, and clinical context evaluation. It covers benchmarks such as multiple-choice, agentic, and multi-turn tests, operational metrics like token usage, and approaches for validating free-text outputs including human review, LLM-as-a-judge, and clinical trials. As LLMs spread into clinical settings, rigorous evaluation is essential to ensure they deliver benefit rather than harm, and this review gives researchers and practitioners a pragmatic reference for matching evaluation methods to their research questions. It addresses challenges unique to LLMs, such as probabilistic and open-ended outputs and behavior that shifts with prompt design and accumulated context, which traditional machine learning evaluation does not handle well. The paper emphasizes that evaluation methods must align with the research question, and it describes underlying concepts and potential pitfalls across all four areas. It distinguishes capability evaluation (benchmarks and operational metrics) from clinical context evaluation (establishing accuracy of free-text outputs via human review, LLM-as-a-judge, and clinical trial approaches).

## [PAIR Companion Agent Sustains Emotional Support Over 14 Days](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.16344) ⭐️ 7.0/10

Researchers deployed PAIR, a theory-based emotion-regulation companion agent, with 19 participants over 14 days, collecting 1,093 sessions that paired emotion estimates with self-reports before and after guided conversations. The study found that guided conversations were followed by higher emotional valence and state-dependent arousal changes, and that perceived helpfulness of the guided conversation significantly increased over time. Most generative-agent emotion support research only tests momentary interactions, so this longitudinal evidence that an agent can remain helpful across weeks addresses a key open question for affective computing and HCI. The findings on cross-session personalization and memory updates could inform the design of future mental-health and well-being companion tools. The agent's emotion estimates aligned more closely with self-reported valence and dominance than with arousal, suggesting arousal is harder to infer from conversation alone. The study links memory updates and retained corrections to cross-session personalization, and emphasizes preserving user control over memory, though the sample was only 19 participants and the work is a preprint.

## [PentestChain: Zero-Cost Automated Pentesting via MCP and Free-Tier LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18120) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PentestChain introduces a ten-phase automated penetration testing framework that combines a deterministic exploit map with a cost-aware AI cascade—starting with a local Ollama qwen2.5-7b model, then free-tier OpenRouter and Cerebras, and a rule-based fallback—all exposed through a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server with eleven tools. The authors report zero measured paid-API cost per engagement and evaluate on AutoPenBench, a Cybench subset, and the PentestGPT 182-sub-task benchmark with multi-trial statistics. Premium frontier models like GPT-4 make continuous automated penetration testing too expensive for the smaller organizations that need it most, so a zero-cost, locally orchestrated alternative could democratize security testing. The paper also treats US-dollar cost per engagement as a first-class evaluation metric and analyzes the attack surface that an MCP-exposed offensive engine introduces. The framework grounds a four-position threat model in the 2025 MCP incident record, including the CVE-2025-6514 remote-code-execution flaw in mcp-remote, the postmark-mcp supply-chain backdoor, and the tool-poisoning/rug-pull/line-jumping class, and contributes four mitigations. On legacy targets measured to date, it detected 26 services and enriched 34 CVEs, though the abstract is truncated before full results.

**Background**: The Model Context Protocol (MCP) is an open standard that lets AI agents connect to external tools and services through MCP servers, making it a natural way to expose an offensive security pipeline to an LLM. Ollama is a tool for running open-source LLMs locally, while OpenRouter and Cerebras offer free-tier API access to hosted models, enabling a cascade that avoids paid inference. Automated penetration testing uses AI to discover and exploit vulnerabilities, but frontier-model token costs have limited continuous testing to well-funded organizations.

## [Graph-RAG vs RAG on Cultural QA: Up to 78% Error Reduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18317) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper benchmarks Graph-RAG against standard RAG on LatamQA, a culturally grounded multiple-choice dataset covering eight thematic categories, and finds that G-Retriever reduces the base LLM's error by 72% with a standard knowledge graph and 78% with a benchmark-aware variant. The graphs were built end-to-end from Wikipedia articles using KGGen, a recent open-domain extractor, without manual curation in the main setting. The work directly addresses the long-tail deficit of LLMs for underrepresented cultures such as Latin America, showing that structured knowledge graph augmentation can be competitive with or complementary to RAG. It offers practical guidance for both the RAG and knowledge graph communities on how to ground culturally specific question answering. The gap between Graph-RAG and RAG narrows as the graph is oriented toward task-relevant content, and the trained projection transfers zero-shot to Portuguese without target-language fine-tuning, indicating multilingual reach. The main setting avoids manual curation, relying entirely on KGGen's automatic extraction from Wikipedia.

**Background**: Large language models often fail on culturally specific facts from underrepresented regions because such facts appear too rarely in pretraining corpora. Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) grounds generation in external text to mitigate this, while Knowledge Graphs (KGs) offer tighter control over what enters the context plus potential gains in explainability and updatability. Graph-RAG combines the two by retrieving from a knowledge graph, and KGGen is a recent tool that uses language models to extract dense knowledge graphs from plain text.

## [ASLEval Reveals Local Privacy Proxies Miss 46.9% of LLM Agent Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18864) ⭐️ 7.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces ASLEval, an authorization-aware framework that measures privacy leakage across full multi-step LLM agent sessions rather than relying on a single local proxy such as a designated action, final response, or attacker report. The authors define 'privacy exposure displacement' and show that an expected-outlet-only view misses 46.9% of the exposure recovered by the union of all declared visible exits. The findings suggest that many current privacy evaluations of tool-using LLM agents systematically undercount unauthorized exposure, which could give enterprises and safety researchers a false sense of security. The paper argues for benchmarks that declare the complete visible boundary, ground claims in pre-specified targets and authorization, and report privacy alongside task utility. Across multiple enterprise-style environments and independently implemented runtimes, the authors observed three recurring patterns: attacker self-reports combine omissions with high false discovery, and schema-aligned internal evidence usually precedes visible exposure at the request/probe level. Reducing model-visible returns can change this path but may eliminate normal-task success, and independent human review supported the adjudication pipeline while flagging harder console and candidate cases.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Tool-using LLM agents operate over multi-step sessions in which they call external tools, inspect intermediate results, and produce final responses, so sensitive data can leak through many different 'exits' rather than a single output. Prior privacy evaluations often inspect only one designated action or an attacker's own report, which may not reflect the true session-level exposure. ASLEval addresses this by pre-registering a hidden target set, measuring all declared visible exits, and reserving internal traces for diagnosis.

## [PACE: Typed Representation and Compiler for AI Cinematic Spatial Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19853) ⭐️ 7.0/10

PACE (Precise AI Cinematic Expression) introduces a typed representation that captures screenplay evidence, characters, props, locations, subject positions, and camera behavior, with values written once at the script, scene, shot, or panel level and inherited below. A compiler converts this representation into both a diffusion-model prompt and a metric 3D scene, and a camera solver places the camera so the declared framing matches the built framing. This work addresses a core limitation of text-to-image diffusion models for filmmaking: free-text prompts leave spatial planning to the model's defaults, making camera placement and scene layout hard to control. By measuring field-by-field how far the compiled camera and staged render deviate from declarations, PACE offers a verifiable, controllable pipeline that could benefit AI cinematography, previsualization, and storyboard generation workflows. On the 11-scene Automatic Drive screenplay, every staged single-subject panel places its subject within 1.2% of frame width of the declared position, but with two or three subjects a single camera pose cannot satisfy every position and the residual is reported rather than absorbed. On 204 external director-storyboard shots, delivered head height is 1.906 times the staged target from the director's words, 1.733 from the compiled prompt, and 0.955 with greybox control; declaring the pose on 30 shots raises the action drawn from 58.9% to 74.4% without moving the framing, while transitions, fitted motion, and human review of generated panels remain open.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Diffusion models are generative models that create images by starting from random noise and iteratively denoising it, and they are widely used for text-conditioned image and video generation. In film production, a screenplay must be translated into spatial decisions such as who stands where and what the camera sees, but text-to-image models typically resolve these decisions implicitly. PACE borrows ideas from compilers and typed intermediate representations, where a program's structure is captured in a typed form before being translated into executable output, and applies them to cinematic planning.

A blog post published on arcturus-labs.com on September 21, 2026 argues that OpenAI is well positioned to fast-follow Jev, the 'System One' model released in limited early access by TypeSafe AI on September 15, 2026 alongside a $40 million seed round led by DCVC. The post sparked a Hacker News discussion with 215 points and 166 comments debating whether OpenAI would or should pursue a similar non-generative, classifier-style model. The debate touches on a core strategic question for the AI industry: whether the biggest labs should chase fast, cheap, non-generative decision models or continue doubling down on reasoning-heavy LLMs. If OpenAI were to fast-follow Jev, it could reshape the market for classifier-style APIs used in inference pipelines, data preparation, and safeguards, affecting both startups like TypeSafe and enterprise buyers. Jev differs fundamentally from LLMs: it does not generate natural-language text but returns typed values with probability estimates and confidence scores, intended to be consumed directly by software, with latency of 70–500ms and pricing at $0.042 per 1M input tokens with free output. Commenters noted that OpenAI's focus on RL-trained reasoning models is the opposite of Jev's design goal of avoiding reasoning entirely for speed, and that adding reasoning on top would sacrifice the price and speed benefits of non-autoregressive output.
