**Background**: TypeSafe AI is a San Francisco-based company founded in 2024 that released Jev in limited early access on September 15, 2026, describing it as the first of a class of 'System One models' that produce typed, calibrated decisions rather than chat text. Unlike large language models that generate token-by-token text, Jev is designed to return choices, scores, or yes/no probabilities that other software can use directly without hallucination risk. The blog post's claim that OpenAI could fast-follow Jev refers to the common big-lab strategy of letting smaller companies validate a new product category before entering with a competing offering.

## [LoRA-Tuned DINOv3 ViT Boosts Underwater Sonar Target Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21061) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21061) adapts DINOv3 Vision Transformers to underwater synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) automatic target recognition using a three-stage framework combining LoRA, hard-negative mining, and supervised contrastive learning. LoRA alone raised AUPRC from 0.300 to 0.679 ± 0.027 with only 0.26% of weights trained at rank 4, while the two refinement stages produced statistically null changes. This work shows that parameter-efficient fine-tuning can bridge the gap between natural-image pretraining and underwater acoustic data, offering a practical path for naval ATR systems constrained by scarce target imagery. The null results for stacked refinement also caution the community that adding complex training stages may not yield gains when the encoder has already fit the data. The evaluation uses at-sea SAS data with a mission-level geographic split, compares all arms at 85% test recall, and repeats each comparison over three random seeds. Hard-negative mining changed AUPRC by -0.0045 ± 0.0119 versus an equal-size random curriculum, and SupCon changed it by +0.0002 ± 0.0096 versus the preceding stage, indicating mining occurred on data the encoder had already fit.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Synthetic aperture sonar (SAS) is an underwater imaging technique used for naval mine detection and target recognition, but deep learning is hampered by scarce labeled target imagery and acoustic clutter that mimics man-made objects. DINOv3 is a self-supervised vision foundation model from Meta that produces high-quality features without fine-tuning, and LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning method that freezes pretrained weights and injects small trainable low-rank matrices. Supervised contrastive learning (SupCon) shapes representations by pulling same-class examples together and pushing different-class examples apart.

## [Ability-Residual Decoupled Framework Tackles Affect Contamination in Cognitive Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21214) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21214) proposes an ability-residual decoupled modeling framework for affective cognitive diagnosis that explicitly captures unmodeled cognitive residuals through student, item, concept, student-concept, and low-rank student-item components before letting an affective module modulate guess/slip effects. A Q-matrix-constrained concept residual attention mechanism adaptively aggregates only item-relevant concept residuals, and experiments on ASSIST2017, ASSIST2012, ASSIST2009, and Junyi with six cognitive diagnosis backbones show response-prediction gains and generally improved affect alignment. This work addresses a subtle but important flaw in affective cognitive diagnosis: the common assumption that a cognitive backbone already explains ability, item, and concept effects, leaving only affect in the residual. By showing that stable cognitive residuals can leak into affective representations and cause 'affect contamination,' the paper offers a more robust modeling pathway that could improve both prediction accuracy and the interpretability of affect estimates in AI in education systems. The framework decomposes residuals into student, item, concept, student-concept, and low-rank student-item components, and uses a Q-matrix-constrained attention mechanism to select only item-relevant concept residuals. Validation includes ablation studies, leakage probes, PCA visualization, long-tail analysis, and case studies, though the paper reports results only on four ASSIST/Junyi datasets and requires affect labels for the affect-alignment evaluation.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Cognitive diagnosis infers students' mastery of concepts from their response logs and is a core technique in educational data mining and intelligent tutoring systems. Affective cognitive diagnosis extends this by incorporating non-cognitive factors such as emotion, engagement, and fatigue, which also influence performance. Item response theory and related models traditionally treat unexplained variance as noise or residual, but this paper argues that systematic cognitive bias can remain in those residuals and contaminate affective estimates.

A new arXiv paper (2609.21390) presents a modular retrieval-augmented architecture that runs offline multimodal large language models in isolated, no-Internet environments to support air operations analysts. In a pilot study with four Brazilian Air Force image analysts, the system matched human performance on an electronic-target identification doctrine test (8/10) and finished in 7.1 minutes versus a human average of 26.5 minutes. This work shows that offline, source-traceable multimodal LLMs can deliver useful decision support in disconnected and security-constrained settings such as military air operations, where cloud-based AI is often unavailable. It also establishes a baseline for comparing manual and AI-assisted workflows, pointing toward broader adoption of local RAG systems in defense and other sensitive domains. The pilot measured cognitive workload of manually producing a reconnaissance target report (REMIR), finding high mental demand (6.0/7) and effort (5.0/7). The paper also outlines a future evaluation protocol to systematically compare manual and AI-assisted workflows, though the abstract is truncated and lacks detailed results beyond the pilot.

**Background**: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) is a technique that lets large language models fetch relevant documents from an external knowledge base before answering, improving factual accuracy and allowing source citations. Offline or air-gapped LLMs run entirely on local hardware without Internet access, which is essential in secure military or enterprise environments where data cannot leave the premises. This paper combines both ideas to give analysts traceable doctrinal knowledge through natural language interaction.

## [Risk-Aware Occupancy Boosts End-to-End Driving Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21470) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21470) proposes risk-aware occupancy, a dense representation that jointly encodes global scene occupancy, map-derived traffic constraints, and future dynamic agent occupancy into a unified BEV map, along with an end-to-end network called ROIDrive that predicts this representation in an independent branch and injects it into planning queries. The authors also introduce the RiskOcc4D-nuScenes benchmark and report relative open-loop collision reductions of 52.9% under the UniAD metric and 35.0% under the ST-P3 metric on nuScenes. Safety in crowded, occluded scenes with unstructured obstacles and uncertain regions remains a core weakness of end-to-end driving systems, and explicitly encoding planning-relevant risk into a unified BEV map offers a concrete way to make trajectory generation more conservative where it matters. If the reported collision reductions generalize beyond open-loop evaluation, this representation could influence how future end-to-end planners fuse perception and planning. The method uses a dense rather than sparse representation, capturing risk evidence in both spatial and temporal dimensions, and is evaluated on the newly built RiskOcc4D-nuScenes dataset derived from nuScenes and Occ3D-nuScenes. The results are open-loop metrics (UniAD and ST-P3), so closed-loop robustness and real-world validation remain unproven, and no code release is mentioned.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: End-to-end autonomous driving replaces the traditional modular pipeline with a single neural network that maps raw sensor data directly to driving plans, but such systems often struggle with stability and interpretability in complex urban scenes. Sparse representations describe the environment as discrete elements like objects and lane lines, which can miss unstructured obstacles and uncertain regions. Occupancy prediction instead assigns an occupancy state and semantic label to each voxel, giving a more general and expressive scene description, and BEV (bird's-eye-view) representations preserve ground-plane spatial relationships, making them well suited for joint perception and planning.

## [RRDrive Adds Risk-Aware Occupancy to Register-Based End-to-End Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21486) ⭐️ 6.0/10

RRDrive introduces risk-aware occupancy (RiskOcc) as a dense, temporally aligned, trajectory-queryable representation for register-based end-to-end autonomous driving, achieving a selected-trajectory PDMS of 0.951 versus the 0.937 baseline (a 1.5% relative gain) and a 7.7% improvement in average candidate PDMS. The authors also release RiskOcc4D-NAVSIM, a dataset with automatic risk annotations, and an external RiskOcc predictor that estimates risk-aware occupancy directly from sensor inputs. The work targets a concrete weakness of register-based planners — sparse scene representations that produce poor candidate trajectories and unreliable ranking — and shows that a dense risk-aware occupancy representation can measurably improve both generation and selection. If the external RiskOcc predictor holds up, it offers a path to deploy this representation without relying on ground-truth occupancy at inference time, which matters for real-world end-to-end driving systems. On the register-based baseline, bad and poor candidates make up 19.74% of the candidate set and the oracle-best candidate ranks only 33.9th on average; in challenging scenes RRDrive improves candidate PDMS by 30.2% and raises the Spearman correlation among good candidates from 0.26 to 0.67. The headline 1.5% relative PDMS gain is modest, and the strongest results are reported in an oracle setting, with the external RiskOcc predictor described only as validating feasibility.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: End-to-end autonomous driving uses a single learned model to map sensor inputs to driving trajectories, and many recent systems generate a pool of candidate trajectories and then rank them. Register-based planners such as DrivoR compress multi-camera features into a small set of camera-aware register tokens, which is efficient but yields a sparse scene representation. Occupancy representations describe 3D space as dense voxel grids, and PDMS (Predictive Driver Model Score) is the standard NAVSIM metric that combines several driving-quality subscores with multiplicative penalties.

## [L2O Proposed as Missing Layer for AI-Native Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21519) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21519v1) argues that Learning-to-Optimize (L2O) should be treated as the missing architectural layer between classical optimization and AI-native intelligence, redefining optimization algorithms as offline knowledge generators that produce supervisory data for neural surrogate models. It introduces a generic four-stage L2O workflow — optimization, knowledge generation, surrogate learning, and runtime inference — and illustrates it with an NR-V2X relay-selection case where MILP-generated solutions train a Graph Neural Network to reproduce near-optimal decisions in real time. If adopted, this framing could shift how AI-native networks are designed, moving optimization from an online decision engine to an offline teacher that trains fast neural surrogates for latency-sensitive network control. It matters for telecom operators, network architects, and ML researchers because it offers a systematic way to transfer optimization expertise into runtime AI models across heterogeneous communication and computing systems. Unlike existing L2O work that mainly focuses on accelerating optimization algorithms, this paper positions L2O as an architectural abstraction spanning heterogeneous systems. The NR-V2X relay-selection example uses a Mixed-Integer Linear Programming (MILP) solver to generate training data for a Graph Neural Network, but the paper is a position/vision piece without demonstrated quantitative results or benchmarks.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Learning-to-Optimize (L2O) is an emerging field that uses machine learning to automate the design of optimization methods, reducing hand-engineering by learning optimizers from training problems. Neural surrogate models are cheaper approximations that mimic expensive simulations or solvers, enabling fast inference. AI-native networks embed AI throughout network functions for autonomous resource management and zero-touch operation, but current architectures lack guidance on how optimization knowledge should be generated and transferred.

## [DSRec: Dual-Interest Cross-SSM Model for Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21548) ⭐️ 6.0/10

DSRec introduces a dual-interest cross-SSM architecture that disentangles long-term and short-term user interests for sequential recommendation, encoding items into long-term interest embeddings via historical aggregation and a short-term branch modulated by inter-click time intervals. It processes these through a full-sequence Mamba encoder for long-term modeling and a time-modulated SSM for short-term intent, with a residual cross-fusion mechanism aligning the two branches, and experiments on public benchmarks show it outperforms state-of-the-art methods. This work addresses two key limitations in sequential recommendation: the cost inefficiency of Transformer-based models and the tendency of existing methods to encode each item with a single static role, ignoring item polysemy. By combining dual-interest modeling with multi-granular SSMs, it offers a more efficient and semantically richer approach that could benefit large-scale recommender systems where both accuracy and inference cost matter. The model uses a full-sequence Mamba for long-term interest and a time-modulated SSM that dynamically adjusts state evolution based on temporal gaps for short-term interest, with a residual cross-fusion mechanism exchanging contextual information while preserving semantic independence. The abstract reports benchmark improvements but does not specify datasets, metrics, or efficiency numbers, so concrete gains remain to be verified in the full paper.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Sequential recommendation predicts the next item a user will interact with based on their ordered history, and Transformer-based models have driven recent progress but are computationally expensive for long sequences. State Space Models (SSMs) such as Mamba offer efficient long-range sequence modeling by evolving a hidden state over time, making them attractive alternatives. Item polysemy refers to the same item playing different semantic roles depending on user context, a phenomenon that static item embeddings fail to capture.

A new arXiv paper (2609.21599) proposes Centroid-Guided Contrastive Loss (CGCL), a loss function that unifies classification with densely formulated clustering to reshape the latent space via a centroid-driven top-k push-and-pull mechanism. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on EMSCAD, a public benchmark for fraudulent job posting detection, and the code is released on GitHub. Fraudulent job postings disrupt the online job ecosystem for both job-seekers and employers, and existing detection methods struggle to simultaneously achieve high accuracy and meaningful latent-space structure. CGCL's unified objective could improve both decision boundaries and clustering compactness, offering a more robust approach for fraud detection and representation learning more broadly. CGCL combines classification with densely formulated clustering through a centroid-driven top-k push-and-pull mechanism, enforcing accurate decision boundaries while maintaining high clustering compactness. The paper reports SOTA results on the EMSCAD benchmark, and the code is available at https://github.com/ali-ahmed925/CGCL_code.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Contrastive learning is a representation learning technique that pulls similar examples together and pushes dissimilar ones apart in an embedding space, often using loss functions like contrastive loss or InfoNCE. Latent space refers to the lower-dimensional embedding space where a model encodes high-dimensional input data, and its structure affects downstream classification. Fraudulent job posting detection aims to identify job ads with fake content, misleading information, or negative intent, and EMSCAD is a widely used public benchmark dataset for this task.

Using a design-based research approach with mixed methods (questionnaires and semi-structured interviews with students and staff at a Higher Education institution), the study found students viewed Beacon's responses as more trustworthy and better aligned with module content than unrestricted generative AI tools. Help-seeking disparities are a persistent equity issue in computing education, where cumulative and cognitively demanding programming tasks make some students hesitant to approach lecturers or tutors. If carefully designed course-specific AI systems can occupy an intermediary space between independent study and formal support, they could broaden access to guidance without replacing educators, which matters for educational technology designers and institutions adopting AI in teaching. Students valued Beacon's use of pseudocode and scaffolded explanations over direct solutions, and viewed it as a valuable first point of support before consulting lecturers or official resources, though they remained cautious about trusting AI-generated responses without verification. The paper is a domain-specific evaluation with no major technical breakthroughs, and the abstract lacks detailed quantitative results.

**Background**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) enhances large language models by incorporating an information-retrieval mechanism that lets models access and use additional data beyond their original training set, which makes it well suited to grounding answers in approved course materials. Design-based research is a cyclical methodology developed with and for educators that seeks to increase the impact, transfer, and translation of education research into improved practice. ## [RAVEL: Retrieval-Aware RL for Interactive Person Re-ID Questioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21924) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces RAVEL, a retrieval-aware online reinforcement learning framework that trains an agent to decide which question to ask next in interactive person re-identification under partial evidence. Unlike prior systems that imitate an offline ordering of candidate QA pairs, RAVEL initializes from supervised question generation, observes the current Top-4 candidates, and optimizes its question policy using rank feedback from the full question-answer-retrieval loop, delivering progressively stronger retrieval performance across five interaction rounds on Interactive-PEDES. This work reframes interactive retrieval as a sequential information-acquisition problem where question value depends on the elicited response and its downstream effect on retrieval, rather than on static heuristics. It matters for interactive search and dialogue-driven retrieval systems, showing that online RL can reallocate questioning budgets toward localized open-ended attributes that yield the largest gains on initially difficult queries. The authors establish that candidate discriminativeness and perceived usefulness provide only weak supervision for the question-selection objective, motivating the shift to online RL. RAVEL observes the current Top-4 candidates directly and optimizes with rank feedback from the full loop; analysis shows it reallocates the questioning budget toward localized open-ended attributes, with the largest conditional gains on initially difficult queries.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Person re-identification (Re-ID) is a computer vision task that recognizes the same individual across different, non-overlapping camera views without relying on facial recognition. Interactive person re-identification extends this by letting a system ask clarifying questions to a user when the initial evidence is partial, turning retrieval into a multi-round dialogue. The Interactive-PEDES benchmark, introduced with the LLaVA-ReID work (ICML 2025), provides data for evaluating such interactive, question-driven text-based person retrieval.

## [ECG Biometrics Tested Under Exercise Stress With Siamese ResNet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21962) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper evaluates ECG biometrics under realistic conditions involving exercise-induced stress and cross-session variability, using a Siamese ResNet with late multi-lead fusion trained on cardiopulmonary exercise test data. It reports an intra-session rest-to-peak equal error rate (EER) of 1.7% and a state-of-the-art 3.9% EER on the public CYBHi dataset. Most prior ECG biometric studies only tested single-session resting data, so this work shows whether cardiac signatures remain reliable when the heart rate and body state change, which matters for authentication and for securing sensitive cardiac data. The results also suggest ECG signals could serve as a pretext task for self-supervised learning on physiological data. The model uses a Siamese ResNet with late multi-lead fusion and is evaluated with an exercise-and-time-aware protocol plus public benchmarks, achieving 1.7% intra-session rest-to-peak EER and 3.9% on CYBHi. The authors frame this as the first extensive assessment of ECG biometrics under combined physiological and temporal variability, and note that robustness challenges remain.

A Siamese network is a neural architecture that learns to compare two inputs and judge whether they are similar, while ResNet is a deep convolutional network with skip connections. Equal Error Rate (EER) is a standard biometric metric where the false acceptance rate and false rejection rate are equal, so lower values mean better accuracy. Researchers present SCR-MF, a modular two-stage workflow that combines scRecover for principled dropout detection with missForest for non-parametric imputation of scRNA-seq data. Across public and simulated datasets, SCR-MF achieves robust and interpretable performance comparable to or exceeding existing imputation methods in most cases while maintaining a competitive balance between accuracy and runtime. Dropout events are a pervasive problem in scRNA-seq that obscure biological signals, so improved imputation methods can directly benefit downstream analyses such as clustering and differential expression. SCR-MF's emphasis on interpretability and computational efficiency makes it potentially suitable for mid-scale single-cell datasets, where practitioners often need a practical trade-off between accuracy and resource use. SCR-MF is a two-stage workflow: scRecover first detects dropout values while keeping real zeros unchanged, and missForest then performs iterative random-forest-based imputation on the detected dropouts. The paper reports that runtime analysis shows a competitive accuracy-efficiency balance, though the abstract does not specify exact benchmark metrics or dataset sizes.

Imputation methods aim to distinguish these dropout zeros from true biological zeros and fill in the missing values. scRecover is an R/Bioconductor package that detects and imputes dropout values while preserving real zeros, and missForest is a widely used non-parametric imputation algorithm that iteratively fits random forests to predict missing entries.

## [LEON Uses Koopman Operators for Latent Transitions in World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27259) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.27259) introduces the Latent Evolution Operator Network (LEON), which realizes latent state transitions in World Action Models through context-modulated operator-based propagation plus additive forcing, grounded in the controlled Koopman generator view of evolution. Across two WAM formulations that couple latent prediction to policy differently, LEON reportedly improves closed-loop performance and robustness, and remains effective even when the transition module is fully replaced. The paper argues that how latent transitions are realized is an architectural choice distinct from predictive representation and prediction-policy coupling, and that this choice materially affects robot policy performance. If the claim holds, it offers the robot-learning community a dynamics-aware alternative to Transformer token-interaction predictors for world-action models, a fast-growing area linking world models, video prediction, and vision-language-action policies. LEON organizes context-dependent transition variation around a shared evolution-operator structure while keeping a complementary additive-forcing path, and controlled dynamical systems are used to verify this evolution-specific inductive bias. The abstract does not report benchmark numbers, compute costs, or comparisons against specific baselines, so the empirical scope remains to be assessed from the full paper.

**Background**: World Action Models (WAMs) extend robot policies by predicting how task-relevant scene states evolve under interaction, and many recent systems do this prediction in a latent representation space rather than generating full appearance-level video. Koopman operator theory describes the evolution of nonlinear dynamical systems linearly by lifting states into a higher-dimensional observable space, and its generator form decomposes evolution into autonomous and control-dependent parts. LEON applies this controlled-generator view to latent WAM transitions, replacing the usual Transformer predictor whose inductive bias centers on token interaction rather than temporal evolution.

## [Study: Advice Style Shapes Judgment of AI Financial Guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20989) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A randomized vignette experiment with 285 U.S. adults across eight financial decisions independently varied three advice styles — AI, expert, and online community — while holding the underlying recommendation constant and displaying source labels. Advice style most strongly shaped message and safety appraisals, expert labels selectively increased perceived source knowledge, and the resulting appraisals explained 69.2% of overall quality, 75.9% of trust, and 82.9% of intended reliance. As generative AI increasingly dispenses personal financial guidance, this work shows that how advice is framed and labeled may matter as much as what it says, which has direct implications for designing financial AI that supports grounded evaluation rather than simply maximizing trust. It also helps disentangle the separate roles of advice style and source labels in shaping appropriate reliance. The experiment used a vignette design in which the same recommendation was shown under AI, expert, or online-community framing, and expert-style advice remained most preferred even when source labels were removed. The explanatory models accounted for 69.2% to 82.9% of variance in downstream judgments, though the sample was limited to 285 U.S. adults and hypothetical scenarios rather than real financial decisions.

**Background**: A vignette experiment is a survey method that presents respondents with short hypothetical scenarios to elicit their judgments and attitudes, allowing researchers to isolate the effect of specific factors. "Appropriate reliance" refers to a person's ability to distinguish correct from incorrect AI advice and act accordingly, a central goal in human-AI interaction research that is distinct from mere trust or reliance. This study applies these concepts to generative AI used for personal financial guidance.

## [Hallucination-R1 Generates Paraphrases That Expose Factual Instability in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21227) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers released HALLUCINATION-R1, a robustness-oriented paraphrase generation framework that uses two-stage optimization to produce semantically faithful yet challenging paraphrases. Experiments on SimpleQuestions, PopQA, and TruthfulQA show it exposes factual consistency failures across multiple model families, and its generated data improves robust accuracy under paraphrase variations in a lightweight fine-tuning study. The work targets paraphrase-induced hallucination, a subtle failure mode where a model answers a factual question correctly in its original form but errs under a semantically equivalent paraphrase. This matters for anyone deploying QA or retrieval systems, since real users rarely phrase questions exactly as in training data, and the framework offers a way to generate harder robustness-oriented supervision. The two-stage optimization first stabilizes meaning-preserving and diverse paraphrasing, then rewards paraphrases that reveal factual consistency degradation in downstream QA models. Analyses indicate the failures are not reducible to surface-level artifacts or semantic drift, and code and models are publicly available on GitHub.

Paraphrase generation is the task of rewriting a sentence while preserving its meaning, and it is commonly used to test or improve model robustness.

## [FOCAL-VLA: Subtask-Guided Geometry Distillation and Implicit World Modeling for VLA Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21228) ⭐️ 6.0/10

FOCAL-VLA is a new framework that combines subtask-guided geometry distillation with implicit world modeling to improve spatial and temporal understanding in Vision-Language-Action models. It transfers geometric knowledge from VGGT by aligning geometry latents with features from subtask-relevant image regions, and uses Track4World features from current and future demonstration frames to capture future 3D interaction dynamics, without running VGGT or Track4World at inference time. Precise, long-horizon robotic manipulation remains a key challenge for VLA models, which often lack sufficient spatial and temporal understanding when mapping 2D observations directly to actions. By focusing geometric and dynamic learning on the current subtask, FOCAL-VLA offers a plausible way to reduce redundant scene information and could benefit embodied AI systems that need reliable real-world manipulation. The method aligns geometry latents from VGGT with subtask-relevant image regions and incorporates Track4World features from current and future demonstration frames, jointly guiding action generation. Experiments reportedly show FOCAL-VLA outperforming baselines on both simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks, though the excerpt does not provide specific benchmark numbers or code release details.

**Background**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models are multimodal foundation models that take an image observation and a text instruction and directly output low-level robot actions, typically built by fine-tuning a vision-language model on robot trajectory data. Geometry distillation transfers structural 3D knowledge from a teacher model to a student model, while implicit world modeling encodes environmental dynamics into latent representations without an explicit prediction module. ## [LoG-VGGT Cuts Memory for Long-Sequence 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21437) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers propose LoG-VGGT, a memory-efficient framework for long-sequence 3D reconstruction that applies cross-window attention at only a small subset of transformer blocks instead of full global attention. It also introduces a global camera consistency refinement module where camera tokens interact with compact register tokens via cross-attention to reduce long-term pose drift. Long-sequence 3D reconstruction is bottlenecked by the quadratic memory cost of global attention and by camera pose drift over long horizons, so a method that bounds memory while improving pose stability could make streaming reconstruction more practical on consumer or edge hardware. It matters to researchers and practitioners in computer vision, robotics, and AR/VR who need scalable scene reconstruction from long video. The design keeps memory bounded by propagating information only across adjacent temporal windows, and the refinement module enforces scene-level constraints across the entire sequence without paying the cost of sequence-wide attention. Experiments report improved depth accuracy and robust camera pose estimation on multiple long-sequence benchmarks, along with competitive streaming reconstruction performance.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Transformer-based 3D reconstruction models typically use attention to relate frames, but full global attention scales quadratically with sequence length, making long videos expensive in memory. Window-based attention, as in Swin and CSWin Transformers, restricts attention to local windows to save computation, but purely local windows can lose long-range context and cause camera pose estimates to drift over time. LoG-VGGT combines these ideas: local cross-window attention for efficiency plus a global refinement step for consistency.

## [Single-Shot Trajectory Warping Wins 2nd Place in HANDS 2026 Dexterous Grasp Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21511) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A team earned 2nd place in the HANDS 2026 Workshop Challenge (Dexterous Grasp Motion track, held with ECCV 2026) by generating grasp motions for the 12-DoF LinkerHand O6 through a single-shot trajectory warping approach. Instead of step-by-step generation, their policy observes the object once and outputs a 12-D warp of a single successful GraspM3 demonstration, which is then replayed open-loop, achieving 94.61% success on the easy track (the highest of all submissions) and 57.18% on the hard track of the private test set. This result shows that reformulating dexterous grasp motion generation as editing a single demonstration can outperform conventional step-by-step policies while avoiding compounding errors, which could simplify how robots learn manipulation from limited data. It also highlights the growing role of large-scale simulation datasets and reinforcement learning in benchmark-driven robotics research. The task requires roughly 70 twelve-dimensional decisions per grasp, and the authors train the warp policy with one-step PPO over all 4,824 training objects in parallel. The approach relies on a single successful GraspM3 demonstration and replays the warped trajectory open-loop, which may limit adaptability if test objects or physical dynamics differ substantially from training conditions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: The LinkerHand O6 is a compact, lightweight dexterous robotic hand used in humanoid and manipulation research, and here it is configured with 12 degrees of freedom for the challenge. GraspM3 is a large-scale dexterous grasping dataset containing over 8,000 objects and roughly one million grasping motion trajectories, which provides the demonstration used by this method. PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization) is a widely used reinforcement learning algorithm that updates a policy in small, stable steps, and the 'one-step' variant here means the policy outputs the entire trajectory warp in a single decision rather than acting at every timestep.

## [Steering LLMs Toward Norwegian Moral Profiles via MFQ-30](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21636) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21636) administers the Norwegian Moral Foundations Questionnaire (MFQ-30) to six open-weight LLMs and compares their foundation profiles against a sample of N = 1,282 Norwegian respondents. It tests two steering interventions — prompt-level persona steering and activation-level ActAdd — finding that a neutral Nordic-respondent persona brings engaging models 44–77% closer to the Norwegian mean in Mahalanobis d², while one-pair ActAdd at a fixed mid-layer flattens the profile rather than steering individual foundations. This work matters because it questions whether psychometric instruments like the MFQ-30 measure anything stable in LLMs, showing that half the tested models produce flat or central-tendency outputs that look near-human on average without actually tracking item content. It also provides a concrete instance of the "cognitive phantoms" warned about by Peereboom et al. (2025), where the same persona that shifts a model's moral profile also induces engagement that was absent at baseline — a caution for cross-cultural LLM alignment and evaluation. Only half of the six open-weight LLMs engaged with the questionnaire under the attention check, while the other half defaulted to flat or central-tendency outputs that appear near-human on average without tracking item content. The neutral Nordic-respondent persona was written without any distributional information from the human sample, and one-pair ActAdd at a fixed mid-layer flattened the foundation profile rather than steering individual foundations.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: The Moral Foundations Questionnaire (MFQ-30) is a validated 30-item psychometric scale measuring five moral foundations — Harm/Care, Fairness/Reciprocity, Ingroup/Loyalty, Authority/Respect, and Purity/Sanctity — each assessed through relevance and judgment items. ActAdd (Activation Addition) is a steering technique that contrasts intermediate activations on prompt pairs (e.g., "Love" versus "Hate") to compute a steering vector that is added to specific layers, enabling reversible behavioral control without retraining. Mahalanobis d² is a covariance-aware multivariate distance measure that accounts for correlations between variables, unlike Euclidean distance, and is commonly used to quantify how far a multivariate observation lies from a reference distribution.

## [New Benchmark Tests LLMs on Chinese Competitive Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21637) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper introduces a dataset and benchmark for evaluating large language models on competitive Chinese-language debate, built from 182 organized matches judged by 120 professional adjudicators using a shared rubric. After filtering incomplete records, the final dataset contains 148 matches, 2,698 stages, and 20,542 exchange units, with three tasks: winner-tendency prediction, stage-score prediction, and best-debater prediction. This benchmark fills a gap in evaluating LLMs on interactive argumentation, where models must track how arguments develop across turns rather than just classify static text. It offers the NLP and AI evaluation community a fine-grained, professionally judged testbed for measuring model agreement with human experts in a culturally specific domain. Zero-shot evaluation of multiple LLMs achieved at most 66.2% winner-prediction accuracy, a highest Pearson correlation of 0.250 between model stage scores and mean human ratings, and at most 56.8% best-debater prediction accuracy. The dataset preserves original stage scores, match votes, best-debater ballots, and adjudication rationales, with manually verified transcripts and segmentation.

**Background**: Competitive debate is a structured contest in which teams argue for and against a motion, and adjudicators score speakers and decide the winner using a rubric. Chinese-language competitive debate often uses a 4v4 format, and adjudication requires tracking how arguments develop through interaction. Existing datasets rarely combine fine-grained debate transcripts with professional judgments collected during real competitions under a shared rubric, which this work aims to address.

## [Successful VLA Policies Converge in End-Effector Geometry, Study Finds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21659) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper analyzes 15,000 closed-loop LIBERO rollouts from four vision-language-action policies, forming 3,600 configuration-matched policy pairs. It finds that pairs where both policies succeed have a median normalized dynamic time warping distance of 0.0120 m, versus 0.0380 m when exactly one policy succeeds, an ordering that holds across every task, every policy pair, and nine sampling and band-limited representations. The result suggests that successful executions converge geometrically across different action interfaces, offering a potential geometry-based signal for evaluating VLA policies beyond binary task success. This could inform how researchers compare, diagnose, and select robot manipulation policies, though the paper cautions that low cross-policy distance does not imply interchangeability. The ratio between both-success and mixed-outcome distances varies severalfold across representations, so the authors report only the direction rather than a fixed multiple; both-failure pairs are more separated but rest on thin, uneven support and are reported as exploratory. A matched baseline still reveals measurable, heterogeneous residual policy differences, and a common 72-action window preserves the ordering while reducing its magnitude.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies map visual observations and natural-language instructions to robot actions, typically by combining a vision-language model backbone with an action decoder. LIBERO is a widely used benchmark of 130 robot manipulation tasks with about 65,000 expert demonstrations, built on the RoboSuite/MuJoCo simulator, and is a common evaluation target for VLA models. Dynamic time warping (DTW) is an algorithm for measuring similarity between two temporal sequences that may vary in speed, making it suitable for comparing end-effector trajectories of different lengths or timings.

## [Wikidata Pipeline Links 4,397 Papers to Archived Source Code](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21667) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper presents an end-to-end reconciliation pipeline that harvests and validates 4,397 ⟨DOI, repository-URL⟩ pairs from software-centric journals JOSS, SoftwareX, and IPOL, plus the SIGMOD ARI reproducibility reports. It defines two Wikidata-based application profiles aligned with schema.org and CodeMeta, enabling rule-based reconciliation at two granularities, and reports that human-reviewed batches have created 4,182 new Wikidata software items cross-linked to their articles. Software is increasingly treated as a first-class scientific object, yet validated links between source code and the scholarly record have largely been missing from the Linked Open Data cloud, isolating archived artifacts from semantic discovery. This work turns Wikidata into a connector between the scholarly record and archived source code, which could improve reproducibility, credit attribution, and discovery for research software across the open science ecosystem. The pipeline uses SWHIDs, Software Heritage's content-addressed identifiers, to anchor software nodes, and a read-only Wikidata lookup showed that only 82 of the harvested repositories were already modeled there. The authors also show that payloads of the emerging COAR Notify protocol map natively onto their input format, so the same backend could later serve a live enrichment stream; all code, application profiles, and datasets are openly released.

**Background**: Software Heritage is a non-profit archive launched in 2016 by Inria and supported by UNESCO that crawls code hosting platforms and package archives, storing source code in a Merkle DAG and assigning each artifact a SWHID. Wikidata is a collaboratively edited multilingual knowledge graph hosted by the Wikimedia Foundation and released under CC0, while an application profile is a set of metadata elements, policies, and guidelines defined for a particular application. COAR Notify is a protocol developed since 2021 that uses W3C Linked Data Notifications to link repository systems with services such as peer review in a distributed, web-native way.

## [BAPA Prevents Entropy-Induced Collapse in Test-Time Binary Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21743) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper theoretically analyzes entropy-induced collapse in imbalanced binary segmentation test-time adaptation, where the larger predicted class dominates updates and produces degenerate masks. It proposes Balanced-Anchor Prompt Adaptation (BAPA), which uses Class-Balanced Anchors and Dynamic Prompt Adaptation to achieve the highest mean Dice across four domains. This work addresses a specific failure mode of entropy minimization, a standard test-time adaptation objective, in dense binary segmentation where class imbalance is common. It offers a prompt-only alternative that could improve reliability in medical imaging, remote sensing, and other imbalanced segmentation applications. BAPA optimizes only text-side prompt residuals while keeping vision-language encoders frozen, refining the foreground-background decision boundary without altering pretrained dense visual representations. Factorized ablations validate the complementary roles of the Class-Balanced Anchors and Dynamic Prompt Adaptation modules.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Test-time adaptation (TTA) allows a model to adapt to unlabeled test data using only its own parameters, and entropy minimization (e.g., Tent) is a common TTA objective that encourages confident predictions. Binary segmentation assigns each pixel to foreground or background, and class imbalance—where one class vastly outnumbers the other—is a well-known challenge. Vision-language models like CLIP enable prompt learning, where soft prompt vectors are optimized for downstream tasks.

## [Matrix AdaGrad: Row-wise and Column-wise Adaptive Subgradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21815) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces a general Online Mirror Descent framework with adaptive proximal functions for matrix-valued parameters, from which the authors derive Row-wise Matrix AdaGrad (Row-AdaGrad) and Column-wise Matrix AdaGrad (Column-AdaGrad). These variants scale updates using accumulated row-wise or column-wise gradient norms, and the authors prove regret bounds that can be strictly tighter than entry-wise AdaGrad under structured gradients. Most widely used adaptive optimizers such as AdaGrad and Adam treat parameters as flat vectors, ignoring the matrix structure that dominates modern neural networks. By aligning adaptive scaling with row and column structure, this work offers a principled path to more stable training at larger learning rates and greater network depths, which could influence the design of next-generation matrix-aware optimizers. The framework derives matrix-aware adaptivity through online regret minimization, with adaptive scaling determined by accumulated row-wise or column-wise gradient norms rather than per-entry norms. Experiments on matrix factorization and deep neural-network training show improved optimization stability and trainability at larger learning rates and greater depths, though the paper is a specialized theoretical advance rather than a broad empirical benchmark.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: AdaGrad is an adaptive subgradient method that scales each parameter's learning rate by the accumulated magnitude of its past gradients, giving smaller updates to frequently updated parameters. Online Mirror Descent is a general online-learning framework that generalizes gradient descent by using a distance-generating function, and its regret analysis provides convergence guarantees. Matrix-aware optimizers such as Shampoo, SOAP, and Muon exploit the matrix structure of neural-network weights, but a general theoretical framework for deriving matrix-aware adaptivity comparable to AdaGrad has been lacking.

## [FedDCN: Federated Deep Clustering for Non-IID Heterogeneous Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21829) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces FedDCN, a generalization of Deep Clustering Networks to the federated setting that simultaneously optimizes a reconstruction loss and a clustering loss. To handle non-IID data across clients, FedDCN generates synthetic data augmentations and adds a geometric regularization term for latent space alignment. Federated deep clustering is still underexplored, and existing methods degrade when client data are non-IID, which is the norm in real-world deployments. FedDCN's approach could make privacy-preserving unsupervised learning more reliable for distributed, heterogeneous data in domains such as healthcare and finance. FedDCN combines a reconstruction loss with a clustering loss and adds synthetic data augmentations plus a geometric regularization for latent space alignment. Experiments demonstrate effectiveness under both IID and non-IID assumptions, and the authors identify future research directions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Deep clustering uses deep neural networks to learn low-dimensional latent representations that are friendly to clustering, and it is typically trained centrally. Federated learning instead keeps data on distributed clients and shares only model updates, but models trained this way often perform worse when client data are non-IID. FedDCN sits at the intersection of these two areas, extending deep clustering to the federated, privacy-preserving setting.

## [Neural Cellular Automata Hidden Channels Enable Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21870) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21870) investigates the internal hidden channels of Neural Cellular Automata (NCAs) and introduces a transfer-learning mechanism that injects a pretrained teacher's hidden states into a student model to guide early optimization. On few-shot and scale-variant MNIST benchmarks, NCAs with only ~9,800 parameters outperform comparable recurrent and feed-forward architectures. This work suggests that NCA hidden-state dynamics can serve as a decentralized, parameter-efficient substrate for transfer learning, offering a path to strong few-shot generalization without the over-parameterization that causes overfitting and memorization in modern deep networks. It could interest researchers working on efficient architectures, continual learning, and biologically inspired computation. Mechanistic analysis shows the hidden channels decouple feature extraction from uniform classification consensus by absorbing morphological complexity and converging to mutually orthogonal states, capturing general scale-invariant topological primitives rather than class-specific templates. This lets a student model achieve strong few-shot performance on unseen classes using features transferred from a teacher trained only on digits 0-5.

**Background**: Neural Cellular Automata combine neural networks with cellular automata: a grid of cells, each updated by a shared learned rule based on its neighbors, enabling self-organizing pattern formation and regeneration. Few-shot learning refers to training models to classify new classes from only a handful of labeled examples, while transfer learning reuses knowledge from a source task to improve a related target task. ## [Benchmarking Explanation Quality of Open-Weight VLMs in Face Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21879) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.21879) proposes a benchmarking framework that treats explanation quality as a core evaluation axis for open-weight vision-language models (VLMs) used in face recognition. It defines two criteria — relevance (reliance on identity-stable facial features) and faithfulness (alignment with visible image content without hallucinated features) — and quantifies them by constraining model outputs to a structured explanation format that supports automated querying and auditing. Forensic face comparison requires transparent and auditable decisions, yet existing evaluations of VLMs focus almost entirely on recognition accuracy while the validity of generated explanations remains unquantified. This work highlights remaining shortcomings in VLM explanations and argues that explanation quality metrics are needed to get a complete picture of model performance, providing a foundation for benchmarking and future fine-tuning of explainable face recognition systems. The methodology constrains model outputs to a structured explanation format so that relevance and faithfulness can be automatically queried and audited, and the authors benchmark several families of open-weight VLMs while jointly evaluating face verification accuracy and explanation quality. The paper also releases an open-source evaluation harness, though the abstract does not specify which model families, datasets, or numerical results were used.

Open-weight models are those whose learned parameters (weights and biases) are publicly released, allowing others to download, run, and often fine-tune them, in contrast to proprietary models. The framework relies on human curation plus VLM generation to construct behavior specifications, and its experiments show that DiaVLo produces behavior labels that correlate with model performance and provide context for that performance. The abstract does not report concrete quantitative results, and the evaluation is limited to several open-source VLMs under classification and generation conditions.

A new arXiv paper (2605.11986v2) evaluates three large language models using three prompting techniques—Zero-Shot, Chain of Thought, and Chain of Thought + Verifier—to automatically generate Entity-Relationship diagrams from natural language requirements of progressively increasing complexity. The results show that while LLMs perform reasonably in simpler scenarios, their reliability drops sharply as complexity grows, with more inconsistencies, ambiguities, and failures to represent constraints. This study provides systematic evidence that current LLMs are not yet mature enough for reliable conceptual database modeling in complex real-world scenarios, suggesting that validation costs may offset any apparent productivity gains. It offers practitioners a cautionary benchmark for deciding when and how to use LLMs in database design workflows. The evaluation qualitatively compared generated ER diagrams against textual requirements, checking structural and semantic adherence of entities, relationships, and attributes. Notably, the study found that adding a Verifier step to Chain of Thought did not prevent the rise in inconsistencies and constraint representation failures as complexity increased.

## [BoB Framework Reweights Benchmark Scores by Semantic Density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30044) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.30044v3) introduces Balance of Benchmarks (BoB), a framework that reweights publicly reported benchmark scores using semantic density weighting, score equating across benchmarks of differing difficulty, and task-relevant residual pooling. Evaluated on 605 configurations across 14 Artificial Analysis benchmarks and on WildScores (148 developer-reported benchmarks), BoB-Support raised family-mean Spearman correlation from 0.764 to 0.823 and cut MAE from 6.19 to 5.10 normalized score points. Uniform averaging of benchmark scores over-weights capabilities that happen to be benchmarked more densely, while manually curated suites discard potentially useful evidence; BoB offers a principled way to retain more benchmark evidence while moderating benchmark proliferation. This matters for LLM evaluation practitioners and leaderboard builders who need fairer, task-conditioned model comparisons from public aggregate scores alone. BoB-Support also improved the three-model shortlist hit rate from 65.3% to 72.6% and reduced retrospective shortlist regret from 2.08 to 1.67 normalized score points, while BoB-Constant reached a Spearman correlation of 0.831 and a 74.6% hit rate. Density weighting additionally reduced average ranking changes when benchmarks are repeated, including as paraphrased copies, and the method relies only on public aggregate scores.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Model comparison increasingly aggregates large collections of publicly reported benchmark scores, but different aggregation strategies trade off evidence coverage against control over capability weighting. Score equating is a standard psychometric technique, used in assessments like the SAT, that places scores from different test forms onto a single comparable scale. Semantic density weighting assigns each benchmark an inverse-density weight based on embedded benchmark descriptions, so nearby entries share aggregate influence at a disclosed density scale.

As multi-agent VLM architectures become more common for complex visual tasks, the lack of shared visual context can cause agents to duplicate work or reach contradictory conclusions. The framework emphasizes that shared visual memory should preserve not only images or textual summaries but also the dependencies among observations, agent interpretations, and subsequent reasoning. ## [NeuSOGA3D: Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Explainable 3D Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.20323) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Researchers released NeuSOGA3D, a hybrid neuro-symbolic framework that reconstructs 3D geometry from unorganized point clouds by projecting them onto principal orthographic planes, building symbolic implicit spline representations, and fusing them via shape-preserving constructive solid geometry (CSG) operations. Experiments across all forty categories of the ModelNet40 benchmark show it can recover structurally meaningful, CAD-compatible representations, unlike conventional neural implicit methods that encode geometry in opaque latent spaces. The work targets a key weakness of neural implicit reconstruction: its latent encodings are hard to interpret and reuse in engineering workflows. By progressively converting observations into explicit symbolic entities such as control polygons, implicit spline fields, cross-sections, and volumetric lofts, NeuSOGA3D points toward explainable geometric intelligence that could feed directly into CAD and design pipelines. The pipeline first builds a coarse visual hull from spline-based observations fused with CSG, then recovers finer detail through cross-sectional decomposition and volumetric reconstruction using Partial Shape-Preserving Splines. It is an arXiv preprint (arXiv:2609.20323v2) with no accompanying code release, and evaluation is limited to the synthetic ModelNet40 point-cloud benchmark.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Neural implicit methods, such as NeRF-style approaches, represent 3D shapes as continuous functions learned by neural networks, achieving high accuracy but hiding geometry inside latent representations. Neuro-symbolic AI combines neural learning with explicit symbolic reasoning to improve reliability and explainability, while constructive solid geometry (CSG) is a classic CAD technique that builds complex solids by combining simple primitives with Boolean operations. NeuSOGA3D sits at the intersection of these ideas, aiming to produce geometry that is both learned and symbolically interpretable.

## [Reinforcement Learning Under Non-Markovian External Influence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.16056) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper establishes conditions under which reinforcement learning in continuous state-action MDPs perturbed by a non-Markovian external process becomes tractable by considering only a finite history of events. It proposes a policy iteration algorithm with policy improvement guarantees and derives sample complexity for least-squares policy evaluation, with specific analysis for discrete-time Hawkes processes with Gaussian marks. This work bridges reinforcement learning theory and real-world scenarios where external events (e.g., market shocks, traffic incidents) violate the Markov assumption, potentially enabling more robust decision-making in domains like finance, robotics, and healthcare. It provides theoretical foundations for handling non-Markovian perturbations, which are common but often ignored in standard RL formulations. The algorithm is not guaranteed to converge globally; instead, policy improvement is guaranteed only in regions of the state space determined by approximation error from using tractable policies and finite histories. The results apply to general discrete-time processes with decaying influence, and experiments demonstrate policy evaluation and deployment in traditional control environments.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning typically assumes Markov decision processes (MDPs), where future states depend only on the current state and action. However, many real-world systems are influenced by external events that introduce non-Markovian dependencies, making standard RL algorithms less effective. This paper addresses this gap by modeling such perturbations and analyzing when finite history suffices for tractable learning.

## [COS-GNN Combines Spiking and Continuous Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.01897) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper proposes Continuous Spiking Graph Neural Networks (COS-GNN), a unified framework that integrates spiking neural networks (SNNs) into continuous graph neural networks (CGNNs) to achieve energy-efficient graph representation learning. It introduces a high-order structure using second-order ordinary differential equations (ODEs) for spiking representation and continuous propagation, and provides theoretical proof that COS-GNN mitigates exploding and vanishing gradients. This work addresses the high computational cost of CGNNs, making them more feasible for deployment on battery-powered devices. By combining the energy efficiency of SNNs with the expressive power of continuous dynamics, it could enable graph learning on edge devices and advance research at the intersection of neuromorphic computing and graph representation learning. COS-GNN employs SNNs for graph node representation at each time step, integrated into the ODE process along with time. The high-order structure uses a second-order ODE to enhance information preservation and mitigate information loss in SNNs, and theoretical analysis shows it captures long-range dependencies between nodes.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Spiking neural networks (SNNs) are artificial neural networks that mimic biological neurons by transmitting information via discrete spikes, offering energy efficiency. Continuous graph neural networks (CGNNs) generalize discrete GNNs by introducing continuous dynamics, often modeled with ordinary differential equations (ODEs), but they require significant computational power. COS-GNN merges these two paradigms to create a more efficient and capable graph learning framework.

## [Controlled Study Isolates Semantic Positive Pairs in Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08722) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2510.08722v4) presents a controlled empirical study that isolates the effect of semantic positive pairs on self-supervised representation learning by constructing two matched ImageNet-1K subsets: an augmented-pair baseline and a manually curated semantic-pair dataset with identical class composition and training-pair count. Across transfer learning and object detection evaluations, semantic-pair pretraining consistently improved generalization over augmented-pair pretraining, with SimCLR showing the largest relative gain among the evaluated contrastive and non-contrastive methods. Previous work often mixed semantic pairs with augmented positives or false neighbors, making it impossible to attribute gains specifically to semantic pairing; this study's matched-dataset design provides cleaner evidence that semantic positives induce invariances beyond standard augmentation pipelines. The findings offer practical guidance for researchers and practitioners designing SSL frameworks, suggesting that contrastive methods—especially SimCLR—stand to benefit most from incorporating same-class instances as positives. The study compares representative contrastive and non-contrastive SSL methods under matched training conditions, and additional ablations show that semantic pairs induce invariances beyond the standard transformation pipeline. Contrastive learning benefits most strongly from semantic pairs, with SimCLR showing the largest relative improvement, though the work is an incremental empirical contribution rather than a new method or breakthrough.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) trains models on unlabeled data by defining pretext tasks; in contrastive learning, a common approach is instance discrimination, where two augmented views of the same image form a positive pair and the model is pushed to be invariant to handcrafted transformations. However, same-image positives can preserve nuisance correlations such as background, texture, illumination, and object-specific details, which may hurt generalization. Semantic positive pairs—different images of the same class—are hypothesized to reduce these correlations by showing objects across diverse contexts, but prior studies rarely isolated their effect. ImageNet-1K is the standard 1,000-class, ~1.28 million-image benchmark subset of ImageNet (ILSVRC 2012) widely used for pretraining and evaluating vision models.

Researchers present BEAT-Net, a supervised biomimetic framework that uses QRS-centered tokenization and a four-stage hierarchical architecture to convert continuous ECG signals into heartbeat sequences for diagnosis. Evaluated on PTB-XL, CPSC2018, and CSN, it reaches 0.924 AUC, comparable to CNN baselines at 0.925 AUC, while cutting parameters by 95 percent from 2.06 million to 0.7 million. The work suggests that embedding explicit physiological structure into model design can rival or beat massive pre-training for clinical ECG tasks, improving data efficiency and interpretability. This matters for healthcare AI deployment where labeled data is scarce and clinicians need decisions aligned with their reasoning. BEAT-Net surpasses the 39.5-million-parameter foundation model HeartLang on morphological Form classification, reaching 0.901 AUC versus HeartLang's 0.832 AUC, and attains full CNN-level performance using only 35 percent of training data. Its learned attention patterns spontaneously align with established clinical heuristics, though it remains a specialized arXiv preprint without community validation.

Most deep learning ECG models treat multi-lead recordings as undifferentiated time-series or images, forcing them to rediscover physiological structure implicitly, which causes data inefficiency and opaque decisions. BEAT-Net instead mimics a cardiologist's workflow by first isolating QRS-centered heartbeats and then processing them through specialized stages, including a Transformer-based Sentence Encoder for global reasoning.

## [CoDRA: A Cost-to-Disturbance Ratio Framework for Robust RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12110) ⭐️ 6.0/10

The paper introduces CoDRA (cost-to-disturbance ratio adversarial training), a framework that expresses the controller-adversary trade-off as a ratio of accumulated cost to accumulated squared disturbance norm, optimized via a self-normalized actor-critic update. On MuJoCo InvertedDoublePendulum, CoDRA achieves the lowest cost at every force level, including forces outside the training range, and in all but one cell of the mass grid. Robust RL is critical for sim-to-real transfer, where policies trained in simulation often degrade on real systems due to unmodeled disturbances. CoDRA's parameter-free approach could simplify robust training pipelines and improve deployment reliability for robotics and autonomous systems. CoDRA scales each value term by a stop-gradient normalization constant computed from the current batch, moderating the adversary's incentive without altering the controller's update and requiring no explicit disturbance penalty or trade-off parameter. Its advantage is less pronounced on the milder InvertedPendulum task, suggesting task-dependent benefits.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Reinforcement learning policies trained in simulation often fail on real systems because they encounter disturbances not seen during training. Robust RL addresses this by exposing the controller to perturbations during learning, using techniques such as domain randomization, adversarial minimax formulations, or probabilistic mixtures of protagonist and adversarial behavior. However, unregulated disturbance mechanisms can destabilize training and collapse nominal performance relative to standard non-robust methods.

## [Robot Skill Evolution as Software Lifecycle: Negative Results and Release Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.07799) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2604.07799v3) reframes long-lived robot skill updates as a software-lifecycle problem, with a fixed planner dispatching versioned skill modules and a runtime shield screening each action. Across six robosuite tasks, the authors report three negative results: peak success is unstable across seeds (23.3% to 73.3% within one method), the four 'modules' are actually whole-task policies rotated on a clock rather than the documented phase decomposition, and the action-discarding shield cut violations 98–100% while driving success to zero. The paper argues that single-run peak numbers cannot reliably rank robot skill methods, which challenges common benchmarking practice in robot learning. Its positive result is that release machinery — a promotion gate and a dip detector — kept all twelve injected regressions out at a 22.5% clean-candidate rejection cost, suggesting governance infrastructure may matter more than the skill modules themselves. At a matched budget, one whole-task policy retained the final-step geometry in 0.734 of episodes reaching it versus 0.023 for the clock rotation, with no seed overlap at four seeds per arm (exact p=0.029); restoring the termination condition the clock replaced raised retention on every seed. The shield's acceptance criterion omitted completions, so a shield that simply stopped the robot scored perfectly, and the dip detector caught nine of twelve regressions but missed all three on one seed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: robosuite is a modular simulation framework built on the MuJoCo physics engine, developed by Stanford's Vision and Learning Lab and later the ARISE Initiative, providing standardized robot manipulation benchmarks such as single-arm tasks. In hierarchical robot learning, a high-level planner or scheduler typically dispatches reusable skill modules, and prior work like RoboMatrix decouples planning, skills, and hardware layers. This paper instead treats each skill update as a software release, borrowing concepts like versioning, rollback, promotion gates, and runtime shields from software engineering.

## [Diagnostic-Guided Model Selection for Retinal Atrophy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16955) ⭐️ 6.0/10

This arXiv paper introduces a task-adaptive diagnostic that decides whether stochastic or deterministic models are appropriate for longitudinal retinal image prediction, based on whether inter-visit change is driven by disease progression or acquisition variability. Applied to an Optos fundus autofluorescence archive of 24,335 images from 9,708 eyes, the diagnostic indicated weak time-dependence, leading the authors to develop Temporal Retinal U-Net (TRU), a deterministic single-pass predictor that outperformed classical and deep-learning comparators on held-out and zero-shot transfer cohorts. The work challenges the assumption that increasingly complex stochastic generative models are always better for longitudinal medical imaging, showing that a simpler deterministic model can suffice when disease signals are weak relative to technical variability. This has practical implications for clinical AI development, where model complexity, data requirements, and interpretability matter for deployment in heterogeneous real-world imaging archives. The diagnostic specifically tests whether inter-visit image change is associated with elapsed time or with time-independent acquisition variability, and the authors found that source-anchored stochastic configurations produced little endpoint-resolvable sample variation. TRU is conditioned on irregular imaging history and prediction horizon, and while it achieved the strongest overall performance, it showed lower precision in the smaller cross-vendor cohort.

**Background**: Fundus autofluorescence (FAF) is a noninvasive retinal imaging technique that captures the fluorescence emitted by lipofuscin in the retinal pigment epithelium, and it is widely used to monitor diseases such as age-related macular degeneration that cause retinal atrophy. Longitudinal modeling aims to predict future images or progression from prior visits, and stochastic generative models have become popular for this task because they can represent uncertainty. However, real-world clinical archives often contain irregular follow-up intervals, varying acquisition settings, and different device vendors, which can introduce technical variability that masks true disease progression.

## [Rhamba: Region-Aware Hybrid Attention-Mamba Pretraining for Resting-State fMRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01240) ⭐️ 6.0/10

Rhamba introduces a region-aware pretraining framework that combines anatomically guided masking with hybrid Attention-Mamba architectures for resting-state fMRI analysis. Pretrained on the ABIDE dataset using region-aligned patch embeddings and three masking strategies (Any, Majority, Pure), it was fine-tuned on COBRE and ADHD-200 for schizophrenia and ADHD classification, with the Mamba-Attention hybrid achieving the highest average AUROC and outperforming state-of-the-art methods. This work shows that combining anatomically informed masking with hybrid sequence models can improve representation learning in neuroimaging, offering a flexible way to balance interpretability, scalability, and performance. It is relevant to researchers at the intersection of AI and neuroimaging who are exploring alternatives to pure Transformer architectures for large-scale brain data. Masking strategy strongly influenced reconstruction loss (Any > Majority > Pure), but this ordering did not translate directly into downstream performance, where differences were modest and dataset-dependent. Peak performance depended on the interaction between masking strategy and architecture rather than a single dominant configuration, and Integrated Gradients was used to identify contributing brain regions.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Resting-state fMRI measures spontaneous brain activity while a subject is not performing a task, and is widely used to study functional connectivity in conditions such as schizophrenia and ADHD. Self-supervised pretraining lets models learn useful representations from large unlabeled datasets by solving synthetic tasks such as masked reconstruction, reducing the need for costly annotations. Mamba is a state space model architecture that offers linear-time sequence modeling as an alternative to the quadratic attention of Transformers, and hybrid designs interleave Mamba and attention blocks to combine their strengths.

## [LiteMedCoT-VL Distills Chain-of-Thought into 2B Medical VLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09384) ⭐️ 6.0/10

LiteMedCoT-VL introduces a pipeline that transfers chain-of-thought reasoning from a 235B teacher model to 2B student models via LoRA-based fine-tuning on explanation-enriched data. On the PMC-VQA benchmark, it reaches 64.9% accuracy, beating the zero-shot Qwen3-VL-4B baseline of 53.9% by 11.0 percentage points and outperforming all published baselines. This shows that a compact 2B model with reasoning distillation can match or exceed models with twice the parameters, making interpretable clinical decision support feasible on resource-constrained portable devices. It addresses the reasoning gap that has limited deployment of medical AI outside well-equipped hospitals. All inference is conducted without image captions by default, simulating a physician interpreting a medical image directly without a radiology report, and visual grounding analysis shows the model relies on image content rather than textual priors. The code is publicly available on GitHub.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Medical visual question answering (VQA) requires a model to combine visual evidence from a medical image with clinical knowledge to answer a natural-language question. Chain-of-thought reasoning lets a model produce intermediate reasoning steps, improving interpretability, while knowledge distillation transfers knowledge from a large teacher model to a smaller student. LoRA is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that adapts large models by training only small low-rank matrices, drastically reducing memory and compute needs.

<a id="item-219"></a>
## [Lightweight Autoregressive Framework Achieves Near Log-Linear Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04287) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv preprint (2606.04287v2) proposes a lightweight autoregressive framework for graph generation that uses structure-guided topological ordering to serialize graphs into regular edge sequences, enabling near log-linear generation complexity. It also introduces a two-phase training strategy combining exploration-oriented augmentation with iterative refinement to reduce overfitting and promote controlled novelty, with experiments showing improved novelty while preserving high validity and uniqueness on molecular and non-molecular benchmarks. Current graph generative models, including diffusion-based and many autoregressive or hybrid approaches, suffer from costly full-adjacency operations, long denoising chains, or at least quadratic complexity, and often merely imitate training graphs rather than generalizing beyond them. This framework's near log-linear scalability and controlled novelty could significantly benefit applications such as molecular discovery, circuit design, and cybersecurity, where generating realistic and diverse graphs at scale is critical. The framework supports both LSTM and Mamba-style causal sequence backbones, and large-memory accelerators enable longer graph-sequence experiments beyond typical GPU limits. However, the abstract is truncated, and the work is a preprint without peer review or community discussion, so full results and reproducibility remain to be assessed.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Graph generation is a core machine learning problem with applications in molecular discovery, circuit design, and cybersecurity. Autoregressive models generate graphs by sequentially adding nodes and edges, while diffusion-based methods iteratively denoise a graph from noise; both families face scalability and novelty challenges. Topological ordering arranges nodes so that edges follow a consistent direction, which can regularize the generation sequence and reduce computational complexity.
