<a id="item-220"></a>
## [New Paper Rethinks Mismatch-First Active Learning for Audio](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13571) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2607.13571v2) critiques the mismatch-first farthest-traversal (MFFT) active learning strategy for frame-level audio classification, showing that every mismatch-first variant performs worse than the plain geometric strategy it builds on under low annotation budgets. The authors propose mismatch-weighted facility location (MW-FL), which spends the entire budget through a disagreement-weighted coverage objective that penalizes similarity among selected segments, and it achieves the best area under the learning curve on two multi-label datasets. This work challenges a prevailing acquisition strategy in active learning for sound event detection, where strong frame-level labels are expensive to annotate. By showing that coverage of selected segments is the dominant factor and that hard disagreement gating is harmful, it offers a more effective and tuning-free alternative that could reduce annotation costs for audio classification practitioners. MW-FL uses the disagreement signal from MFFT to obtain nonnegative weights for a facility-location objective, applying fixed smoothing without dataset-specific tuning. Experiments across two geometric mechanisms and three ways of using disagreement show that soft disagreement weighting helps on top of coverage, while hard disagreement gating of selection is harmful on both mechanisms.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Active learning selects the most informative unlabeled samples for annotation to maximize model performance under a limited labeling budget. In frame-level audio classification, such as sound event detection, models must assign labels to each audio frame, making annotation especially costly. MFFT was a popular strategy that combined classifier disagreement with diversity via hard sequential decisions, but this paper shows its design is blind to similarity among selected segments.

<a id="item-221"></a>
## [New metric pinpoints which model drives diversity in LLM ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00285) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2608.00285v2) introduces a per-model dissent contribution metric, defined as the complement of a model's mean similarity to the other ensemble members, to measure where diversity originates in language model ensembles. In an experiment with sixteen models from ten families producing 7,082 formulations of psychotherapeutic vignettes, the ensemble achieved an average semantic diversity of 1.69 distinct formulations versus a single-model baseline of 1.43. This work addresses a gap in ensemble evaluation for open-ended tasks where no single answer is uniquely correct, such as clinical case formulation, by moving beyond aggregate diversity scores to identify which model contributes the most divergent perspective. It could help practitioners design more effective ensembles and better understand whether diversity is a property of the ensemble or of individual models. The study used the Vendi Score, the exponential of the von Neumann entropy of a similarity matrix, as the aggregate diversity measure, and preregistered a hypothesis that model identity accounts for a non-zero share of variance in dissent. Results showed that model identity was a detectable structuring factor, but scale differences pointed in opposite directions across pairs, family grouping only held on five two-member lines, and the most divergent voice changed with panel composition, meaning the surfaced outlier describes the ensemble rather than the model.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Language model ensembles combine outputs from multiple models to provide diverse perspectives, but measuring diversity is challenging when there is no single correct answer. The Vendi Score is a recently proposed metric that interprets diversity as the effective number of distinct elements in a sample, based on pairwise similarities. This paper extends that idea by attributing diversity to individual models, which is important for understanding ensemble behavior in subjective tasks.

<a id="item-222"></a>
## [PAVE Combines Predictive Learning and Value Guidance for Robot Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30378) ⭐️ 6.0/10

PAVE introduces a direct world-action policy that combines outcome-agnostic predictive learning with outcome-aware policy improvement, adding trajectory-relative multi-horizon transition alignment at 25%, 50%, 75%, and 100% of the remaining episode on top of a fixed-offset JEPA objective. It also trains an independent distributional value critic on deployment trajectories, converts action-chunk-aligned N-step advantages into positive, negative, or null text conditions, and uses them to guide a flow-matching actor. Behavior cloning is the dominant recipe for vision-language-action policies, but it cannot distinguish useful dynamics from low-quality demonstrations, so PAVE's ability to extract learning signal from every valid trajectory while keeping direct online action generation could make robot policies more robust without adding inference-time overhead. This matters for robotics teams that must train on mixed-quality real-world data rather than curated expert datasets. The multi-horizon predictor and value critic are training-only components and are removed from online execution, so the deployed actor still generates actions directly from the current observation, language instruction, and proprioception. The paper reports the strongest overall performance across three simulation benchmarks, but it is an arXiv preprint with no real-robot validation or community discussion reported.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Vision-language-action (VLA) policies map visual observations and natural-language instructions directly to robot motor commands, and recent systems such as RT-1 and RT-2 showed that transformer-based policies benefit from large-scale data. Most such policies are trained by behavior cloning, a supervised imitation-learning method that copies expert actions but does not evaluate whether a trajectory was good or bad. JEPA (Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture) is a self-supervised approach that predicts abstract embeddings of future observations instead of reconstructing raw pixels, which is the predictive backbone PAVE builds on.

<a id="item-223"></a>
## [TabScope adaptively selects localized or full-table reasoning for table QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03395) ⭐️ 6.0/10

TabScope introduces a question-adaptive framework that dynamically decides between localized sub-table reasoning and full-table reasoning for table question answering, using operation-aware table decomposition to build question-specific sub-tables. The authors also introduce silver reference sub-tables for evaluating evidence selection and construct SLQA, a benchmark based on real-world long tables, with experiments on WikiTQ and SLQA showing that adaptive selection achieves the best overall performance. Large language models degrade in accuracy as tables grow, and this work shows that the right fix is not always localization but deciding when to localize, which could improve long-table QA systems used in enterprise and analytical settings. It also contributes a new benchmark (SLQA) and an evaluation method for evidence selection, giving the community better tools to measure progress on long-table reasoning. The framework predicts the question type and uses it to choose the reasoning mode, with localization proving especially effective for lookup and local reasoning questions while questions needing broader evidence still benefit from full-table reasoning. The abstract is truncated and the code and datasets are only promised upon publication, so full reproducibility details are not yet available.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Table question answering (Table QA) is the task of answering natural-language questions over structured tabular data, and modern approaches often rely on large language models that read the table as text. Because long tables contain much irrelevant content, a common strategy is localization: extracting a smaller sub-table that likely holds the answer. TabScope builds on this idea by asking not only how to localize but whether localization is appropriate for a given question at all.

<a id="item-224"></a>
## [CPR framework brings continuous autoregressive modeling to piano rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18216) ⭐️ 6.0/10

A new arXiv paper (2609.18216v2) introduces the Composer–Performer–Refiner (CPR) framework for prompt-conditioned piano MIDI-to-Music rendering, combining continuous autoregressive modeling with local flow matching and full-sequence refinement. It also proposes Bottlenecked Representation Alignment (BREPA) and Modality–Time RoPE (MT-RoPE) to strengthen musical semantic structure and cross-modal temporal alignment, with code released on GitHub. This work addresses the trade-off between discrete-codec autoregressive models, which suffer quantization loss, and flow matching/diffusion models, which incur high full-sequence attention costs. By operating directly on continuous representations, CPR could offer a more efficient and detail-preserving paradigm for expressive music rendering, benefiting researchers and practitioners in music generation and audio synthesis. The framework uses Composer to autoregressively predict continuous hidden states, Performer to generate 24kHz acoustic latents via local flow matching, and Refiner to upsample the waveform to 48kHz. BREPA and MT-RoPE are introduced to improve semantic structure in hidden states and temporal alignment across modalities.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Prompt-conditioned piano MIDI-to-Music rendering aims to synthesize audio that faithfully plays target notes while matching the timbre of a reference recording. Existing methods mainly follow two paradigms: autoregressive modeling, which often relies on discrete audio codecs and can lose acoustic detail through quantization, and flow matching or diffusion, which preserves acoustic structure but requires costly full-sequence attention and may weaken semantic structure. Continuous autoregressive models operate directly on continuous representations, potentially combining the strengths of both while avoiding quantization.

<a id="item-225"></a>
## [CoReLoop Boosts Cross-Domain Audio Deepfake Detection via Recurrent Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19818) ⭐️ 6.0/10

CoReLoop introduces a parameter-efficient recurrent refinement method that reuses the outputs of a frozen self-supervised learning (SSL) based audio deepfake detector, training only lightweight refinement modules and loop-specific low-rank adapters. On 14 cross-domain test sets, it reduces the pooled equal error rate (EER) from 4.85% to 3.74% with two passes, using roughly 10M trainable parameters out of 598M. Audio deepfake detection struggles to generalize to unseen attacks, and this method improves cross-domain robustness without collecting new data or altering the original detector's parameters, making it practical for deployment. It also shows that parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques like low-rank adapters can be effectively applied to audio security tasks, potentially lowering adaptation costs for real-world systems. The method preserves the detector's original first-pass prediction and adds an optional halting head that selects refinement depth per utterance, achieving 3.73% pooled EER with an average of 1.18 passes. A diagnostic showed that naively recycling encoder outputs as inputs degrades detection, so CoReLoop adapts recurrent inputs to the frozen encoder, controls state updates, and aligns refined outputs with the frozen classifier.

rss · ArXiv ML (cs.AI) · Sep 22, 04:00

**Background**: Self-supervised learning (SSL) speech models learn rich acoustic representations from unlabeled audio and are widely used as backbones for audio deepfake detection. Equal error rate (EER) is the standard metric for these detectors, measuring the point where false acceptance and false rejection rates are equal. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) is a parameter-efficient fine-tuning technique that trains small low-rank matrices instead of updating all model weights. CoReLoop combines these ideas by adding recurrent refinement loops on top of a frozen SSL detector.
